#3135. Prediction: $5,000 Invested in Palantir Will Be Worth This Much by 2031

Investors should expect considerable price action with this data analytics stock in the next five years, but will that help them?

Palantir (PLTR -9.73%) has become a phenomenally successful investment for longer-term shareholders, but also one that is difficult for new buyers to jump into. The stock is up about 25-fold from its December 2022 low. However, its elevated valuation also makes it risky to buy at current levels.

Long-term investors often buy stocks planning to hold them for a minimum of five years. Given that, it may be useful to speculate about where this stock could trade in 2031 and where that outcome would leave the value of a $5,000 investment made in it today. Although a lot could happen during that time that would dramatically alter any forecast we make this month, I think investors should prepare for significant struggles alongside the company's growth. Here's why.

Trying to figure out what history says about Palantir can be challenging, though. From a certain perspective, the business can justify the stock's premium. The stock has been a tremendously successful investment because the company has delivered real productivity gains to its clients, particularly on the commercial side of its business.

One example is Citibank's CitiWealth, which used Palantir's software to streamline the onboarding process for new customers. This reduced the onboarding time for the most complex accounts from nine days to seconds. Now, a single employee can handle everything related to opening an account. Previously, 50 were involved in the process.

Also, the U.K.'s National Health Service is using Palantir's software to plan staffing, monitor patient demand, and track where hospital beds are available. This allows for more efficient resource allocation, reducing bottlenecks and shortening wait times.

Such productivity gains helped Palantir increase its revenue by 92% year over year in the first nine months of 2026. Due to a more modest rise in its costs and expenses, its profits during that time surged by 201% to almost $3.1 billion.

Though the AI data analytics company has been growing impressively, any forecast about what its growth might look like over the next five years will require a large dose of guesswork. The average forecast among analysts today is for an 84% revenue increase this year, then a deceleration to 51% growth in 2027. Grand View Research forecasts a 31% compound annual growth rate for the overall artificial intelligence industry through 2033. Since we do not have company-specific revenue forecasts for Palantir beyond 2027, that might be an appropriate estimate for its growth in the period ahead.

If we project a 52% rise in 2027 and a 31% increase each year thereafter, from projected revenue of $8.55 billion in 2026, the top line would rise to $38 billion by 2031.

However, investors have to remember that Palantir's current price-to-sales (P/S) ratio is 55. Considering that tech growth stocks often support P/S ratios in the teens or 20s, paying such a premium is likely risky in today's market.

Additionally, dividing Palantir's current $373 billion market cap by the projected $38 billion in revenue gives the stock a forward 2031 P/S ratio of 10. That sales multiple is on the lower end of what some tech growth stocks may command but remains significantly above the current S&P 500 average of 3.7.

Furthermore, investors tend to punish stocks for slowing growth, even when their prior growth rates were unsustainable. That is unlikely to bode well for Palantir stock over the next five years and, at least in the short and medium term, could put pressure on its stock price.

That said, Palantir deserves a valuation premium given its growth rate, and annualized revenue increases of 31% would arguably warrant that higher P/S ratio. Hence, investors should not expect that premium to completely disappear.

Amid the uncertainty, I expect that a $5,000 investment in the SaaS stock made today will be worth nearly the same five years from now.

Thanks to a massive premium in the stock price, investors who invest in Palantir today can expect the shares to face significant headwinds over the next five years. Most, if not all, of the revenue and earnings gains the company will make over that period have already been priced into the stock.

Admittedly, that is a somewhat conservative estimate. If the company's revenue growth does not decelerate to 31% as quickly as I anticipate, or if Palantir maintains a higher valuation premium, an investment in it could increase in value.

Nonetheless, investors should not expect the stock to maintain a 55 P/S ratio over time, particularly as its revenue growth slows. Although we can't know the extent of its future growth slowdown and multiple compression, such actions will likely pressure Palantir stock over the next five years.

Thus, even if that projection for the price of Palantir stock to stay essentially flat turns out to be conservative, investors should probably temper their expectations for its share price growth over the next five years.

#3134. IRS Announces 2027 Income Tax Brackets, Updated Standard Deduction

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Here's a rundown of changes to some well-known tax provisions for the 2027 tax year.

Each year, the IRS updates common tax provisions, such as income tax brackets and the standard deduction, to ensure the tax code is keeping pace with the rising cost of living.

These tweaks — formally known as inflation adjustments — help prevent “bracket creep,” where taxpayers who received a cost-of-living raise can pay more in taxes even though their purchasing power remains unchanged.

Inflation adjustments for the 2027 tax year were set at 3.2% — larger than the previous two years as inflation heated up. This could mean lower tax bills for some people when they file in 2028.

In the U.S., there are seven marginal rates — 10%, 12%, 22%, 24%, 32%, 35% and 37% — which will remain unchanged for 2027.

Contrary to a popular myth, it’s uncommon for people to pay a single flat federal tax rate on their taxable income. Instead, portions of a person’s income can be taxed at different rates depending on which tax brackets they fall into. For example, some of a person’s income could be taxed at 10%, another chunk at 12%, and so on.

For 2027, the IRS is adjusting those tax brackets — the windows of income that dictate where a tax rate ends and begins — for each filing status. The adjustments mean that some people may be able to keep some of their income in lower tax brackets, and those who received a cost-of-living raise may avoid getting a portion of their income pushed into a higher bracket.

Married filers, for example, can make up to $104,050 to remain with a top tax rate of 12%, compared with $100,800 in 2026. This increase could allow some couples to shelter an additional $3,250 from a higher tax rate in 2027.

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Single filers

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Heads of household

2026 vs. 2027 tax brackets: Married filing separately

» MORE: See the full 2027 tax brackets for each filing status

The IRS lets most filers lower their taxable income by taking the standard deduction or itemizing their returns. The standard deduction, a flat amount based on filing status and age, is popular, as it requires less work to claim and is often more beneficial for those who might not have deductible expenses.

For 2027, the standard deduction will be $16,600 for single filers and those married filing separately, a $500 increase from 2026. Joint filers will be eligible to take $33,200 and heads of household can take $24,925, a $1,000 and $775 increase over 2026, respectively.

» Need help deciding whether to itemize or take the standard deduction? Learn about the differences

Also included in the IRS' 32-page inflation release are various updates to several tax provisions beyond the federal tax brackets. Plus, a brand-new tax credit will go into effect in 2027.

Starting in 2027, taxpayers who contribute to a health flexible spending account (FSA) can contribute up to $3,500 and, if their plan permits, carry over up to $700 into the next tax year.

For those with health savings accounts, the 2027 limit for contributions will rise to $4,500 for self-only coverage and $9,000 for family coverage.

The annual exclusion for gifts, which limits how much taxpayers can give an individual without filing a gift tax return on certain gifts, will be $20,000 per person in 2027, an increase of $1,000 compared with 2026.

The lifetime estate tax exclusion establishes a threshold for the taxation of estates upon a person's death. In 2027, estates valued at or below $15.48 million will not be subject to estate tax, up from $15 million in 2026.

The Education Freedom Tax Credit will launch on Jan. 1. According to the IRS, it aims to support private contributions for K-12 scholarships. Taxpayers who donate to a "scholarship granting organization" in one of the 30 participating states can receive a nonrefundable tax credit of up to $1,700 ($3,400 for couples filing jointly).

#3133. Moelis: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Moelis's private capital advisory unit now spans all seven of its GP-led secondaries Managing Directors, widening its reach into sponsor liquidity work.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Moelis (MC -4.84%) announced Q3 revenue of $426 million. The investment bank's deal pipeline expanded as corporate clients sought strategic mergers and acquisitions (M&A) to build scale. Three things management said stand out.

Corporate demand for mergers and acquisitions remains elevated as companies seek scale and growth. Moelis expands its mandate pipeline even as market volatility creates timing uncertainty for capital markets transactions.

The firm continues building out its private capital advisory (PCA) unit, which helps private equity sponsors manage liquidity and execute secondary transactions. Adding specialized talent allows Moelis to win mandates across both deal advisory and continuation funds.

Recruiting experienced senior bankers strengthens coverage across high-fee sectors including technology, financial institutions, and industrials. Expanding senior headcount positions the firm to capture larger market share as transaction activity accelerates.

Client advisory activity remains active across corporate advisory and debt restructuring despite macro headwinds, and leadership reflects that operational momentum in its outlook.

A broad suite of advisory services provides structural insulation when public issuance slows, enabling the firm to capture capital-structure advisory mandates during market shifts. Management is closely monitoring transaction timing and advisory fee conversion rates.

#3132. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

At least 80% of assets provide exposure to companies in the S&P 500.

Ultimately, the sensible choice depends on your convictions, diversification, and willingness to accept the risk of turning the Musk spigot off entirely.

#3125. Breakfast News: Staying Calm in a Correction

Where our analysts see opportunity in the AI sell-off.

When stocks fall this fast, it’s tempting to assume they’ll keep falling, and acting on that fear is often costly.

Three of our analysts offer a more measured view of the AI sell-off, each through their team’s philosophy. Yasser El-Shimy and Sanmeet Deo bring the Rule Breakers lens of patience with bold, long-term bets, while Matt Frankel brings Hidden Gems’ disciplined but flexible approach to putting cash to work. All three also share stocks on their radar right now.

Read on for how each of them is approaching the decline!

Yesterday felt like free fall: the Nasdaq Composite sank nearly 5%, erasing Wednesday’s post-election rally and putting the index on pace to close in correction territory, as Nvidia (NVDA -9.26%) and Micron (MU -17.46%) led a broad AI/chip sell-off.

As David Gardner, our Chief Rule Breaker, likes to say: “The stock market always goes down faster than it goes up, but it always goes up more than it goes down.” For Rule Breakers, the only term that counts is the long-term. Yes, political scrutiny may crimp AI capex growth. But a slower build-out likely extends the cycle rather than compressing it into three or four manic years — bullish, in my view, for patient investors willing to buy into the irrational selling likely over the coming days and weeks. This is a marathon, not a sprint.

One stock I’m eyeing after the sell-off is Shopify (SHOP -6.06%), whose price chart says something broke even though the business numbers say otherwise. Shopify fell about 8% after posting $119 billion in GMV, up 29%, revenue up 32% to $3.7 billion, and monthly recurring revenue up 19%, then slid further as today’s tech selloff dragged every highly-valued stock down with it. Buried under the carnage are signs of a resilient business that thrives in an AGI era: AI-driven traffic and orders to merchant storefronts tripled year over year, and Shopify is quietly becoming checkout infrastructure for the agentic web.

This is why Meta Platforms (META -3.96%) partnered with the Canadian company to power Muse AI’s e-commerce infrastructure. Of course, a stock near 70 times earnings leaves little room for error. But I’d call this a valuation reset, not a verdict on execution. I welcome further price declines on this business, because the last quarter makes me want to add more to my already sizable position.

It has been a rough few weeks in the stock market, especially when it comes to AI-related stocks like chipmakers and infrastructure names. The Nasdaq is now in correction territory, falling 10% from its October high, and in many cases the selling has little to do with how well the underlying businesses are doing, and is driven by fears related to the AI build-out and how it's being paid for.

Of course, nobody knows where the bottom is. It's entirely possible that stocks could fall further before things get better. But for long-term investors, paying lower prices means paying less for the future earnings of solid businesses, and there are some excellent businesses that trade for valuations we haven't seen in a while.

On Team Hidden Gems, we try to stay disciplined but flexible, and sell-offs like this one are when that approach pays off. We keep some dry powder on hand and watch our Potential Growth Indicator (PGI), which compares the cash sitting in money market accounts with the total value of U.S. stocks. When fearful investors retreat to cash, the PGI rises, and we get more eager to buy great businesses at lower prices.

One AI infrastructure play in particular I'm looking at is Comfort Systems (FIX -11.58%) which makes HVAC systems for commercial buildings, particularly data centers. Its backlog nearly doubled year-over-year in the third quarter, but the stock has fallen more than 50% from its peak and trades for less than 17 times forward earnings, compared with its five-year average of 26.

Another on my radar is Alphabet (GOOGL -2.20%)(GOOG -1.93%), which had its worst day in a long time after it reported recent earnings, even though Google Cloud's revenue more than doubled and overall revenue grew by nearly 25%. Concerns about Google's advertising results are fair and are worth watching, but at the current price, Alphabet trades for less than 15 times training earnings, far below other mega-cap tech companies.

It’s been an unsettling roller coaster in the stock market the past few days and your emotions may be running hot. This volatility is the price of admission to ride what we call investing. An important aspect of investing I have learned in my career is patience. If I lacked it, I missed out on some very big winners and was left with regret. Warren Buffet once said that “The stock market is designed to transfer money from the impatient to the patient.” Have you initiated that transfer or are you managing your emotions? A good first step would be to take a couple minutes to take long exhale breaths to calm the nervous system. Then, you trust the habits and mindsets you have built to make patience natural.

The Rule Breaker Investing recipe for patience starts before you buy: commit to holding at least three years, so inevitable drops don’t trigger fear. Put on the jersey and stick with companies you love, like fans cheering through a bad season. Expect a rollercoaster, but remember this ride ends on a mountaintop, with the market’s roughly 10% long-run return at your back. Ignore minute-by-minute wiggles. Be a winter soldier, not a sunshine patriot who flees at the first storm. And lean on structure over willpower: own plenty of stocks, size positions to your sleep number, buy in thirds, and review quarterly, not daily.

Owning plenty of stocks also means looking beyond AI. SharkNinja (SN -0.97%), which just reported earnings, is top of mind for me right now. It delivered excellent sales growth, surging net income, and expanding gross margins, and it sells products customers use daily that won’t be affected by AI, data centers, or capex spend. While it won’t completely soothe the red in our portfolios, it’s a reminder to make sure our portfolios consist of a diversified group of companies that reduce exposure to one hot sector.

And in the ultimate power move to face your fears, find a stock you have wanted to own for a while and just buy a few shares (nothing crazy) and let it ride. Now the impatient investor has transferred money to you. You’ll feel better that you were able to zig while the market zags.

Which stocks from your watchlist are you eyeing now that valuations have pulled back?

#3124. Banks are set to report this week. Here's what the options market expects

Bank earnings kick off this week with no fewer than seven of the biggest names set to report. With the S&P 500 near all-time highs, the results could provide a significant market catalyst.Financials are the third-worst performing S&P 500 sector in 2026. Despite being flat on the year, the options market is pricing in some big moves as investor weigh the impact of higher rates and stretched consumer.

Here's what the options market sees, according to the latest Cboe LiveVol data.Goldman Sachs - Tuesday, Oct. 13

The options market is pricing in a 2.84% move for Goldman Sachs after it reports earnings. Across the last eight reported quarters, the stock has moved just about 2% on average. Only three of those quarters have seen a negative stock reaction, with the most recent being a 1.87% move lower in April.The stock rallied a whopping 9% after July's Q2 report, which is the largest post-earnings move since January, 2019.JPMorgan - Tuesday, Oct. 13

Options are pricing in a move similar to Goldman Sachs for JPMorgan, with a 2.5% implied move inline with its past eight quarter average of 2.22%. Unlike Goldman, JPMorgan's recent reports haven't been met with much positivity. The stock's 2.5% move higher following July's report was its first positive reaction since April, 2025, when it jumped 4% higher.Citigroup - Tuesday, Oct. 13

Citigroup heads into Tuesday's report up 11% on the year, best performance of any of this week's reporting names. Options are pricing in a tame 3% stock reaction in either direction, well below the 3.8% it has averaged across the last eight reported quarters. There is cause for pause, though. Last quarter, the stock surprised to the downside to the tune of 5.3% against a 3.5% implied move.Wells Fargo - Tuesday, Oct. 13

Wells Fargo rounds out Tuesday's big bank earnings as a name with a lot to prove. Not only is the stock down 10% this year, but it has also flunked its last three earnings reports, including a 5% move lower in April. This time around, options are implying a 3.5% move in either direction. That's well below its 5.6% average across the last eight quarters.Bank of America - Wednesday, Oct. 14

The options market thinks Bank of America might be able to sneak into positive territory on the year after Wednesday's report. A move higher would be its third-straight post-earnings win. The current implied stock move is 2.9% in either direction, meaningfully above the 1.8% swing it has averaged across its last eight reported quarters.Morgan Stanley - Wednesday, Oct. 14

This company has rarely put a foot wrong in its recent earnings history. The stock has only moved lower once in its last eight reported quarters, a 1.3% move lower in July, 2025. This time around, options are implying a 3% move in either direction, compared to its eight-quarter average of 4.3%. A move higher on Wednesday would make it five straight for Morgan Stanley.BlackRock - Wednesday, Oct. 14

A move higher would also be five in a row for BlackRock, which jumped nearly 7% following July's report. Its last negative reaction came last July – a 6% tumble, This time, options are implying a 3.3% move in either direction. That's tame compared to its 4.4% average across the last eight quarters.

#3121. Arlo’s Third Quarter: A Good Story, Priced on 1 Launch

Arlo's near-two-thirds recurring mix and 49% non-GAAP gross margin show why the subscription engine matters more than hardware sales.

On Nov. 5, Arlo Technologies (ARLO -2.50%) told investors that its security cameras are becoming a subscription business. That is mostly true. The harder question is whether the next leg of growth is as certain as management made it sound.

Arlo sells cameras at a loss on purpose, then collects monthly fees at high margins. Subscription and services revenue hit a record $96.0 million, up 20%, and made up 64.4% of revenue, with non-GAAP services gross margin of 83.9%. GAAP, or Generally Accepted Accounting Principles, is the official accounting standard. Non-GAAP is management's adjusted version. Paid accounts reached 6.6 million, up 23%.

That is a real, durable machine. The call's three claims, though, each come with a footnote.

ARR, or Annual Recurring Revenue, is subscription revenue annualized. It is $369.9 million, growing 14.5%. The 2027 target requires doubling that growth rate.

The bridge is Comcast, ADT, and a new elder-care business. Comcast is the big one. Management has framed its 31 million broadband households as a potential opportunity comparable to Arlo's European alarm partner, Verisure. But the launch has already slipped toward early 2027, subject to field testing.

The filings add a wrinkle. Paid accounts are growing faster than ARR, so revenue per account has fallen roughly 7% in a year, by this analysis. More customers are paying less each. Quarterly ARR additions also shrank, from about $8 million in Q2 to about $5 million in Q3.

The share is accurate, but part of the reason is that hardware shrank. Product revenue fell from $59.6 million to $53.1 million while total revenue grew about 7%. Services gross margin also slipped, from 85.1% to 83.9% on the non-GAAP basis. The call offered no explanation.

Profit. GAAP operating income was $1.0 million on $149 million of revenue. Gross profit rose $12.9 million year over year, but operating expenses rose $12.7 million. The gain was absorbed.

The main culprit is stock-based compensation, or stock paid to employees instead of cash. It was $21.7 million in the quarter versus $13.1 million a year ago. Over nine months, the company repurchased $44.4 million of stock and still saw shares outstanding rise from 105.0 million to 107.5 million. A new $100 million buyback was authorized anyway. Free cash flow of $50.6 million over nine months falls below zero once $63.2 million of stock comp is subtracted.

One more item to watch. Channel inventory at U.S. retailers rose to 14.6 weeks from 9.6, and at U.S. distributors to 14.9 weeks from 5.9. Q4 revenue guidance of $137 million to $147 million sits below Q3.

The investment thesis survives, but it narrows. Arlo has no debt and $144.0 million in cash and short-term investments, so the downside is cushioned. The upside, however, rests largely on one partner launch arriving on time and on revenue per account stabilizing.

At roughly $13 before the print, about 3.4 times ARR on enterprise value, the stock is fairly priced if growth stays in the mid-teens and attractive only if the 30% target proves real. Three signals matter most: the Comcast launch date, ARR per account, and whether buybacks finally shrink the share count.

The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Arlo Technologies. The Motley Fool recommends Comcast. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3117. Mattel: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Barbie's sales are lagging, and management is pivoting capital toward the core toy business rather than unproven corporate bets.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 5, 2026, Mattel (MAT -3.85%) revised its full-year net sales outlook downward to 1% to 4% growth in constant currency. Leadership transitions and slowing store demand test whether core toy assets can drive steady financial performance. Three things management said stand out.

Hot Wheels demonstrates how Mattel scales core product lines into broader ecosystem sales. By expanding into building sets like Mattel Brick Shop, the business opens new revenue streams beyond traditional die-cast vehicles.

Barbie remains Mattel's primary doll brand, but lower sales highlight the need for consistent execution. Management is sharpening the product lineup and targeting adult collectors to revitalize demand.

New leadership is directing capital toward internal projects that generate cash returns above internal hurdle rates. Investments must strengthen primary toy operations before funding unproven corporate initiatives.

Softer retail-store trends across major markets are pressuring management to maintain pricing discipline and align factory production with reduced full-year revenue targets. Leadership watches late-quarter purchasing activity while maintaining brand investments.

Long-term value relies on using core toy brands to expand high-margin entertainment and digital experiences. Operations focus on disciplined execution to deliver consistent shareholder returns.

#3115. UnitedHealth shares are doing something unusual ahead of earnings

The only thing more expensive than health insurance is perhaps the costs of insuring shares of health insurers. Case in point: UnitedHealth Group, which reports earnings Tuesday.

Implied volatility is running at twice realized volatility ahead of the October 13 quarterly earnings release. In other words, options prices - the cost of insuring the underlying stock - are expensive, which sets up well for short premium strategies.

In the case of UnitedHealth shares, traders can sell November premium to cut the cost of a longer-dated January call by more than half. UnitedHealth heads into Tuesday's report with much of the damage from its prior missteps already priced in. While rising healthcare costs always remain a headwind, management's 13% to 16% EPS growth target looks achievable. The stock has basically been flat over the last five years.

By the way, according to the company, 90% of United's 52 million customers worldwide are in the U.S. By its own statistics, the company covers about 12% to 13.5% of the U.S. population.

Growing enrollment is critical to EPS growth, but so too is underwriting margin. In the managed care and health insurance business, underwriting margin represents the core operational profitability generated directly from managing healthcare risk. It measures the financial return an insurer achieves strictly from collecting premiums and managing medical care. On the enrollment side, ACA marketplace-driven enrollment appears poised to decline, a headwind for revenue in a segment that had been a growth driver. Fortunately, it's a smaller piece of the enterprise than Medicare and Commercial. Commercial fee-based enrollment grew ~2.9% YoY between 2024 and 2025, while total Medicare enrollment grew 4.5%. The commercial segment growth may not look impressive as a headline number, but given the U.S. workforce grew ~1.4% over the same period, United appears to be gaining market share.

At 17 times forward earnings, and with free cash flow at 5.7% of enterprise value and 6.5% of market cap, the stock isn't priced for any recovery. On the options side, the market is implying 40% volatility for a stock that's been realizing 20%. Granted. The 40% number captures this week's earnings, and it's likely elevated because the stock has had a couple of eye-watering earnings moves in the past 8 reported quarters. In January, the stock fell almost 20% following earnings, and April 2025 saw a similar sharp decline.

As it stands, UNH is about 12-13% below the midpoint of the two-year high and low. The stock has traded lower in five of the last 8 earnings. Current options prices imply a 6.5% earnings move, according to Cboe LiveVol data. That's the second highest implied move in almost two years.

Sell the November 340/420 strangle to help finance the purchase of the January 2027 400 call. Buying the Jan 400 call costs $18.20. Selling the Nov 420 call at $5.80 and the Nov 340 put at $4.80 brings in $10.60, for a net debit of about $7.60, cutting the call's cost by roughly 58%. If UNH finishes November between 340 and 420, both short legs expire worthless and the January call is held for $7.60, with a breakeven of $407.60 at January expiration. A rally through 420 is covered by the long 400 call. The real risk is below: a close under 340 means buying shares there, for an effective basis near $347.60 including the debit.

#3113. Democrats Retake the House and Senate: Here's What It Could Mean for SpaceX and Tesla

Democrats won control of Congress during the midterms, including winning key races in Texas.

Following the 2026 midterm elections, Democrats captured both chambers of Congress and won several closely watched statewide races. Perhaps one of the bigger surprises came from Texas, where Democrats won the governor's office and a Senate seat for the first time in decades.

For investors following Tesla (TSLA -10.77%) and Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -9.05%), the election results are particularly interesting. Both companies have operations in Texas, and their futures are increasingly tied to emerging technologies that could face more regulatory oversight.

Here's how the changing political landscape could affect Elon Musk's businesses.

For most of its history, Tesla was marketed as a pioneer for electric vehicles (EVs). But in recent years, Musk has been vocal about his vision in which autonomous driving, robotaxis, and humanoid robots become major contributors to the company's business model. These ambitions come with a different set of regulatory standards that traditional automakers don't necessarily face.

In September, the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) opened an investigation into whether Tesla's Cybercab complies with federal vehicle safety standards following its commercial deployment in Austin, Texas.

This investigation highlights a distinction between Tesla's existing EV business and its emerging autonomous transportation platform. Selling cars is one thing, but operating a large fleet of driverless vehicles introduces questions about passenger safety and accident reporting.

With Democrats now in control of Congress, lawmakers could pursue more comprehensive legislation around safety standards for autonomous vehicles. Such rules could increase Tesla's compliance costs or slow down the pace of its robotaxi expansion.

On the other hand, a more robust national regulatory framework could benefit Tesla in the long run by replacing inconsistent state-level requirements with more predictable standards.

For investors, the question isn't whether more regulation is inherently good or bad. It's whether Tesla can still commercialize its autonomous vehicle technology quickly enough to justify the growth expectations reflected in its valuation.

Although SpaceX operates in a different industry than Tesla, its long-term ambitions are largely tied to government oversight. The company's Starship rocket is a key component of its plans for reusable space transportation and, eventually, orbital data centers.

Expanding launch operations requires approval from the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA). Last month, the FAA announced plans for an environmental impact review covering Starship's launch and landing operations at SpaceX's Starbase facility in South Texas.

While this may seem like a nonstarter on the surface, this process is actually pretty important because SpaceX's strategy depends on increasing launch frequency while also improving the unit economics of reusable rockets.

In theory, a more demanding environmental review process could lengthen approval timelines and increase the company's operating expenses. Nevertheless, there is an important counterargument.

Remember, SpaceX provides launch services for NASA, the Department of Defense (DOD), and commercial satellite operators. Its capabilities support national security, scientific research, and America's broader objectives around space exploration. With that in mind, additional regulatory oversight doesn't necessarily translate into reduced demand for SpaceX's services.

The election results in Texas add an interesting dimension to this analysis. Democrat Gina Hinojosa defeated incumbent Republican Governor Greg Abbott, while James Talarico won the state's U.S. Senate race. Democrats also gained control of the Texas House, although Republicans retained the state Senate.

For Tesla and SpaceX, this introduces the possibility of changing priorities at both the state and federal levels. Tesla's headquarters and some of its major manufacturing operations are located in Austin. Meanwhile, SpaceX's Starbase complex is near Boca Chica.

While Texas lawmakers may choose to revisit policies involving autonomous vehicle safety, environmental permitting, and industrial development, the governor does not have the authority to unilaterally rewrite state law. Meanwhile, Republicans' continued control of the Texas Senate could limit sweeping legislative changes. Investors should also remember that Democrats' congressional majority doesn't necessarily guarantee new federal regulations, since President Trump retains veto powers.

Ultimately, I don't think the midterms fundamentally undermine the long-term growth opportunities for either Tesla or SpaceX. Tesla still has several manufacturing facilities around the world, while SpaceX's competitive position in launch operations and satellite infrastructure remains strong outside of Texas.

The midterms have not changed what Tesla and SpaceX are trying to accomplish in the long run. They may, however, change the regulatory environment and pace at which those ambitions unfold.

Adam Spatacco has positions in Tesla. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Tesla. The Motley Fool recommends Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3105. These 6 Utility Stocks Offer Income and a Link to Power Demand

NextEra Energy is leaning into electricity demand, but it isn't the only way to play demand growth.

NextEra Energy (NEE -0.23%) and Dominion Energy (D -0.16%) plan to merge, as NextEra seeks to tap into the massive growth in electricity demand it expects. Duke Energy (DUK +0.38%), The Southern Company (SO +0.24%), American Electric Power (AEP -1.91%), and Xcel Energy (XEL -0.16%) are all hoping to do the same thing. And there's a huge opportunity, with electricity demand expected to increase 60% between 2025 and 2045. To put that demand into perspective, it increased by only 10% between 2005 and 2025.

Here's a look at each of these six large dividend-paying U.S. utilities and why you might want to consider buying each one.

NextEra Energy offers a 2.8% dividend yield. That's in line with the industry average of roughly 2.8%. However, NextEra is anything but average; it is the world's largest utility by market cap. And it plans to get even larger through the proposed acquisition of Dominion (a utility discussed in more detail below).

But the real story with NextEra is that the business has two parts. The foundation is its regulated utility assets, today largely composed of its Florida Power & Light operations. In addition, NextEra operates one of the largest wind and solar power businesses on the planet. This business uses long-term, market-based contracts. That has been an important growth engine for the company. If you are looking for a single utility investment, NextEra covers a lot of ground.

Duke Energy offers a 3.3% yield. In recent years, it has become a leaner business, selling its contracted power assets to focus on its regulated operations. It operates across the electric and natural gas segments of the industry, with regulated utilities in North Carolina, South Carolina, Florida, Indiana, Ohio, and Kentucky. It is also one of the world's top 10 largest utilities by market cap.

Duke believes its $100 billion capital spending plan over the next five years will support annual earnings growth of 5% to 7%. The company generally has constructive relationships with its regulators, which increases the likelihood of success on this front. And in many regions where it operates, there are mechanisms that automatically adjust rates based on spending. It is a solid option for investors seeking a regulated utility.

The Southern Company has a yield of 3.2%. It has either increased or maintained its dividend for 79 years, making it a highly reliable income stock. The most notable event in the company's recent history was the completion of Vogtle 3 and 4, the newest nuclear power plants built in the United States. That sets the company up well to supply the electricity demand that it sees ahead.

That said, the company has an $81 billion capital investment plan that it believes will support long-term earnings growth of 7% to 8%. Like Duke, the company has electric and gas operations. Its regulated electric business spans Alabama, Georgia, and Mississippi, with a smaller, unregulated business that sells power under long-term contracts. Its modest natural gas distribution operations expand the company's reach into Illinois, Tennessee, and Virginia. Like NextEra and Duke, it ranks among the world's 10 largest utilities.

American Electric Power offers a 3% yield. It is projecting annual earnings growth of 7%-9% through 2030, powered by a $78 billion capital investment plan. And, like the three companies above, it ranks among the world's 10 largest utilities.

The company has an extremely broad footprint, with regulated electric operations across 11 states: Arkansas, Indiana, Kentucky, Louisiana, Michigan, Ohio, Oklahoma, Tennessee, Texas, Virginia, and West Virginia. It also has a large transmission business. It is probably one of the most diversified electric utility options here.

Like Duke Energy, Dominion Energy has been slimming down to its core utility operations. The most recent move was to sell the majority of its regulated natural gas utilities to Enbridge (ENB -1.37%). But there's an even larger change on the horizon, as Dominion has agreed to be acquired by NextEra Energy. Dominion's yield is 3.8%.

The big draw for NextEra Energy is twofold: Dominion has a large nuclear power business, and it operates in one of the world's most important data center markets. Notably, buying Dominion will catapult NextEra into the number two spot in nuclear power in the U.S. market. It will also expand NextEra's business to include Virginia, North Carolina, and South Carolina. That said, Dominion is really a special-situations play at this point given the pending acquisition, so it probably won't appeal to most investors despite its relatively high yield.

Xcel Energy yields 2.9%. It recently updated its five-year capital investment plan, extending it through 2031. The update includes $70 billion in capital spending that management believes will drive earnings growth of 9% or more. That's particularly high for a utility.

Notably, Xcel operates in regions well-suited to developing solar and wind power. That gives it an edge as the world shifts toward renewable power generation. With operations across eight Western and Midwestern states, Xcel is probably best suited for investors who favor growth.

Electricity is an increasingly important source of power. In fact, the modern world can't work without reliable electricity. All six utilities on this list provide reliable power, though each does so slightly differently and in different regions. If you are looking to generate income from the increasing demand for power, any one of them could be a good starting place for investor preferences that range from growth to income to special situations.

Reuben Gregg Brewer has positions in Enbridge and Southern Company. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Enbridge and NextEra Energy. The Motley Fool recommends Dominion Energy and Duke Energy. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3102. Don't let inflation bite — how to hedge with TIPS, commodities and infrastructure funds

The Consumer Price Index hit 3.7% in September – the latest sign that inflation remains a problem that isn't going away.

It's a tough situation for investors looking for protection, but there are some good ways to hedge.

Long-term Treasury Inflation-Protected securities (TIPS) yield 2.9%, near multi-decade highs. Beyond TIPS, analysts suggest commodities, infrastructure, and other assets that can hedge against rising prices.

While inflation is down sharply from its 9.1% peak in 2022, it's still well above the Federal Reserve's 2% target. Headline CPI's 3.7% rate in September topped estimates for 3.6%. Core CPI, excluding food and energy, reached 2.6%, also higher than expected. Rising energy prices drove much of the increase. But the Personal Consumption Expenditures index, the Fed's preferred gauge, also remains elevated at a core 3% rate.

Americans' inflation concerns are also rising. The New York Fed's Survey of Consumer Expectations showed the one-year inflation outlook in September climbed to 3.9%, its highest level since May 2023.

Despite their name, TIPS haven't provided much inflation protection this year. The iShares TIPS Bond ETF, which tracks the ICE U.S. Treasury Inflation Linked Bond Index, has a total return of -1.16% year-to-date.

Like most other bonds, TIPS have been hit hard by rising rates. The 10-year Treasury note is up sharply this year and spiked Wednesday to 5.29%, near the highest levels since 2002. Bond prices and yields move inversely, and as rates climbed many TIPS funds suffered.

But higher yields have made TIPS more attractive. The 10-year TIPS' 2.9% yield is up from roughly 1.9% early in the year.

"Given where rates are, it's getting interesting," says Jason Draho, head of asset allocation Americas at UBS Global Wealth Management.

TIPS pay a fixed coupon, or interest rate, but as inflation moves higher, the principal value of the bond increases. The adjustments reflect changes in inflation, based on the non-seasonally adjusted CPI for All Urban Consumers, with a three-month lag.

The upshot is that TIPS investors earn interest on a higher principal value over time. That's the essence of the protection compared to traditional Treasuries.

TIPS' poor performance this year isn't because of a surge in inflation expectations. Almost all the increase has been due to rising real yields — the nominal yield minus expected inflation. Real yields are up because of factors like robust economic growth and a U.S. fiscal deficit around 6% of gross domestic product (requiring far more government borrowing).

If nominal rates were rising just due to inflation, then TIPS total return — a mix of yield and price — would be fine, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. But that isn't the case.

"TIPS have produced losses in total return because their income accruals have been insufficient to offset the price decline produced by the rising underlying real yields," Gallo explained.

TIPS maturing in 10 years or more are highly sensitive to duration risk, Draho notes. "You're making a big call on rates not rising any further if you buy TIPS, especially longer maturities of 10-year plus," he said.

The bull case is that inflation comes down quicker than expected. That's not the market's expectation, based on short-term Treasury yields, notes Draho. But if inflation does ease next year, which he expects, the yield curve would adjust lower.

One way to manage duration risk is to own individual TIPS rather than a fund that has to maintain duration consistent with a benchmark index. Investors could create a TIPS bond ladder with varying maturities, pegged to their income needs over the next decade, for instance.

If you hold a TIPS to maturity you may experience price volatility, but you'll get the original principal or inflation-adjusted price, whichever is greater, when the bond matures.

Short-term TIPS provide more protection against rate increases and they look attractive, said PIMCO portfolio manager Greg Sharenow.

"While as a firm, our view is that inflation will ultimately come down, that view keeps on being pushed out as real world events unfold," he said. "We've seen over and over again the last five to seven years — where we're now in our third supply shock — that you end up actually underpricing versus realized inflation," he added.

Funds focusing on short-term TIPS include Vanguard Short-Term Inflation-Protected Securities ETF, iShares 0-5 Year TIPS Bond ETF and PIMCO 1-5 Year US TIPS Index ETF.

TIPS, of course, aren't the only way to go. Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, suggests a multi-pronged strategy, including equities, commodities, and alternative assets.

"I'd start layering in more income, keep interest-rate exposure moderate, and then what a lot of people are doing is use things like alternatives to to create more balance in a portfolio," he said. "That makes a lot of sense to me."

Broad inflation-protection funds include State Street Multi-Asset Real Return ETF, Fidelity Strategic Real Return Fund and PIMCO Inflation Response Multi-Asset Fund.

The PIMCO fund's holdings include TIPS, commodities, gold and real estate investment trusts.

"The idea is to have a number of different exposures so that we can have a more balanced return and not try to be so specific in getting the exact right asset class at any one moment," said Sharenow, a manager on the fund.

He adds that he sees "significant value" in TIPS, which have the heaviest weighting in the portfolio. Commodities are the next largest holding. They have price "pass-throughs," which is how much companies can shift costs to customers through price increases. That is beneficial to investors, he noted.

Separately, the fund holds about 10% each in gold and REITs.

Draho recommends commodities, based partly on long-term asset-allocation studies that include the 1970s when inflation was far higher. "Over a long period they can help reduce risk and improve diversification," he said. "Their benefits have been most pronounced when inflation was overshooting expectations."

Rather than picking individual commodities, he suggests owning a basket that's consistently rebalanced. Gold and oil, for instance, perform well in different economic and monetary policy scenarios, and it can be tricky to get the timing right.

Commodity ETFs to consider include the Invesco Optimum Yield Diversified Commodity Strategy ETF (PDBC) and the iShares S&P GSCI Commodity-Indexed Trust (GSG).

Another asset class Draho suggests is infrastructure — operators of roads, ports, bridges, and other hard assets. Companies in the space tend to be toll takers, collecting long-term revenue under contracts that include inflation riders or built-in price adjustments.

Investors can get exposure to infrastructure through ETFs such as the iShares Global Infrastructure ETF (IGF) and ProShares DJ Brookfield Global Infrastructure ETF (TOLZ).

Draho isn't sounding alarms on inflation but says it makes sense to add protection against another move higher.

"The risk now is there will be supply side shocks that could push up inflation again," he said.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#3100. MercadoLibre Plunged After Earnings. Here's Why I'm Not Selling.

The Latin American e-commerce and fintech leader grew revenue 45%, but higher spending squeezed its margins.

Shares of MercadoLibre (MELI -12.05%) fell by 12% on Nov. 5, the day after the company's third-quarter earnings came in below analysts' expectations. That leaves the stock more than 40% below its all-time high. Of course, some of it was due to broader market pressure, as the Nasdaq Composite had its worst day in years, but there were also numbers in the company's results that drove the decline. MercadoLibre is one of the largest investments in my own portfolio, so naturally, I'm not thrilled about the drop. But I'm not planning to sell a single share.

MercadoLibre's revenue grew 45% year over year to $10.75 billion, or 41% on a currency-neutral basis, slightly ahead of analysts' estimates. Its commerce business grew revenue 47%, and its fintech business grew 42%.

The underlying metrics were strong, too. Gross merchandise volume (GMV), the total value of goods sold on its marketplace, rose 33% on a currency-neutral basis. Total payment volume through Mercado Pago grew 53% to $109 billion, and the fintech platform now has about 93 million monthly active users, up 29% from a year ago.

In a nutshell, the problem was profitability. MercadoLibre earned $8.83 per share in the third quarter, below the roughly $9.30 analysts expected, and its operating margin shrank to 6.3% from 9.8% a year earlier.

Based on that, you might be wondering whether something is going wrong with the business, but I don't see any massive red flags. MercadoLibre increased its fulfillment capacity by almost 20% from the second quarter, offered free shipping on more orders, and increased marketing spending in Brazil. As CFO Martín de los Santos explained on the call, "The quarter when you are building capacity, you don't have the revenues yet, so you have the cost." He also said he expects margins to recover next quarter.

But the two biggest reasons MercadoLibre's operating margin fell year-over-year were higher operating costs and bad debts from the rapidly growing credit portfolio.

Speaking of the credit portfolio, it's also worth noting that side of the business held up. MercadoLibre's loan portfolio grew 62% to $17.9 billion, and the rate of loans 15 to 90 days past due was 7%, roughly unchanged from the prior quarter and close to historical lows, according to management. Management also said it's being a bit more conservative with new loans in Brazil and Argentina, which I like to see when a loan book is growing this quickly. There were higher rates of bad debt, but that's a function of the rapid growth of the credit portfolio, not of deteriorating credit quality.

I own MercadoLibre because it's the dominant e-commerce and fintech platform in Latin America, it's still growing far faster than most large companies, and its logistics network would be very difficult for a competitor to replicate. Nothing in this report changes any of that. In fact, the biggest reason profits came in light is that the company is widening that logistics advantage. After the drop, the stock trades for less than 40 times forward earnings, well below its five-year average of about 63 times. Source

To be sure, there are risks. If free shipping and heavier marketing turn out to be permanent costs rather than investments, margins could remain lower for longer. And a lending portfolio growing 62% a year would be the first place to show stress if Latin American consumers hit a rough patch. I'll be watching both closely, especially whether margins recover next quarter as management expects.

Having said all that, a company growing revenue 45% doesn't become less valuable because it chose to spend more in one quarter. If anything, I'm more likely to add to my position than to sell.

Matt Frankel, CFP® has positions in MercadoLibre. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends MercadoLibre. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3096. Here's How Many Shares of This Unstoppable Dividend Stock You'd Need for $10,000 in Yearly Passive Income

It's beating the market by more than triple this year.

Dividend stocks are an important component of a well-diversified portfolio at any time, and Coca-Cola (KO -0.07%) is one of the best. It's a Dividend King, which means it has raised its dividend for at least 50 years, and it has one of the best track records on the list, with 64 consecutive increases. That's reliably growing passive income.

But getting $10,000 annually in dividends from Coca-Cola stock may require a bigger investment than you realize.

Coca-Cola is crushing the market this year, up 38% versus the S&P 500's (^GSPC -4.36%) 12% gain. It's been performing phenomenally despite inflation, and investors see the stock, which has been a longtime favorite of investing legend Warren Buffett, as a safe haven against artificial intelligence (AI) stocks.

However, the high stock price means the dividend yield is lower than usual, because price and yield move inversely. Today, Coca-Cola's dividend yield is 2.2%.

It pays $2.12 in annual dividends per share as of its February increase, so you'll need 4,717 shares to reach $10,000 in annual dividends. At the current price, 4,717 shares would cost $452,926.

That's nearly half of $1 million in Coca-Cola stock alone. Unless you have much more than that to invest in a variety of dividend stocks, it makes more sense to spread your money around and benefit from other dividend stocks instead of concentrating too much in one stock.

Jennifer Saibil has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3092. Traders pile into 'bubble puts' on CoreWeave as neocloud stocks plunge

A key corner of the artificial intelligence trade is taking a beating on Wednesday and traders are betting it could get even worse.

Shares of neocloud companies – businesses that offer access to the GPUs powering artificial intelligence as a service – plunged Wednesday after debt yields for Nvidia-backed CoreWeave continued to reach new heights. (An article in The Financial Times that the company is targeting insurance companies for further debt financing apparently didn't help things either.)

Meanwhile, in a post on X, Social Capital founder Chamath Palihapitiya raised questions about a private credit bubble alongside a screengrab showing CoreWeave's bonds hitting a 15% yield. Elon Musk replied "yikes." CoreWeave stock plunged 15%. Nebius Group and Iren, popular trades connected to the data centers build-out, also fell as much as 10%.

Options volume in CoreWeave surged to more than seven times the 30-day average, Nebius almost four times, and Iren twice the normal volume. More calls traded than puts in Nebius and Iren, but in both, more calls were likely initiated by sellers than buyers, according to Cboe LiveVol data.

The most bearish action was in CoreWeave, where traders bought just shy of 200,000 puts, compared to 106,000 calls.

One standout trade happened around 10 a.m. ET when someone opened a new position of 26,000 37.5-strike puts expiring Dec. 18 that traded near the ask price, suggesting the puts were purchased, according to SpotGamma data.

"These are bubble puts," said Scott Bauer, CEO of Chicago-based Prosper Trading Academy. "These are not rip-ups that you buy thinking they'll go to zero. This is the hub, the center of the AI trade, and likely some kind of margin play for someone to hedge their exposure in highly correlated stocks or maybe even the Nasdaq."

The contracts sold for 74 cents apiece for a total of $1.9 million and need CoreWeave to erase another 42% over the next two months to pay off. Prior to this trade, there were just over 1,000 open contracts, meaning the position is a new one.

CoreWeave IPO'd in March 2025 at $40 per share.

"Maybe this is someone that was in early or in on the IPO saying this is my breakeven level," Bauer added. "Maybe they couldn't get this kind of protection in other stocks, the index, or other products."

#3089. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

#3086. Teva's 3 Key Branded Drugs Grew Sales 33% Last Quarter. Is This Turnaround Stock a Buy Right Now?

Teva's three branded therapies of Austedo, Ajovy, and Uzedy brought in $1.1 billion in third-quarter revenue.

Teva Pharmaceuticals (TEVA -0.17%) has increased annual revenue for three consecutive years, following a stretch in which annual revenue fell for six consecutive years.

The culprits for the Israel-based pharmaceutical company's six-year slide were increased competition from generics, pricing pressures, and the loss of exclusivity for key products. Thanks to an emphasis on higher-margin therapies, the company appears to be back on track. Its shares are up more than 34% so far this year.

The road back may still be a bumpy one. In the third quarter, revenue was $4.4 billion, down 2% year over year. However, there are plenty of positive signs.

The company's pivot-to-growth strategy involves moving toward more innovative brand-name treatments and complex biologics, away from the company's former bread-and-butter of generics.

Combined sales across its three main innovative growth drivers, including tardive dyskinesia treatment Austedo, migraine therapy Ajovy, and schizophrenia treatment Uzedy, rose 33%, year over year, to $1.1 billion for the quarter. That helped offset the company's generics sales of $2.16 billion, down 16% from the same quarter a year ago.

The new drugs helped Teva improve gross profit margin from 51.4% in the same period a year ago to 55.5%, while non-GAAP gross profit rose by 345 basis points to 58.7%, the highest it has been in a decade.

The company is in the process of launching another promising branded therapy, Weltruza, a once-monthly subcutaneous injectable for adults with schizophrenia. It was approved on Oct. 9 by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA). The company estimates there are roughly 2.2 million people in the U.S. with undiagnosed schizophrenia, with an annual sales potential of $1.5 billion to 2 billion.

Ecopipam, a treatment for pediatric Tourette syndrome, is expected to be approved in the first half of 2027. It affects nearly 100,000 worldwide. The company is also hoping to launch the DARI asthma inhaler as early as next year.

Teva completed a major equity milestone by terminating its legacy American Depositary Shares (ADS) program and transitioning to direct trading of ordinary shares on the NYSE on Sept. 14. This conversion removed extra depositary fees for investors and streamlined foreign tax handling, bringing Teva into line with standard U.S. corporate equity structures.

Teva is dual-listed on the Tel Aviv Stock Exchange, but the NYSE serves as its primary market liquidity engine. The NYSE listing gives U.S. institutional funds, active asset managers, and retail investors direct, frictionless access to trade the equity in U.S. dollars.

Maintaining high visibility on the world's largest stock exchange reinforces the company's identity as a global biopharmaceutical leader rather than just a regional domestic player.

Teva reached its original 2027 target of $800 million in annual biosimilar revenue a year early, in 2026. Expanded partnerships, including its recently announced commercialization agreement with Samsung Bioepis for biosimilars to Remicade and Enbrel, are establishing a durable secondary pipeline to backstop generic erosion.

The company spent $297 million on research and development in the third quarter, up 16% year over year, with the payoff being a more robust pipeline of biosimilars, as well as immunology and neuroscience therapies.

The company has 15 biosimilars in its pipeline and says it has the potential to develop more than 31 by 2030.

Since 2019, Teva has paid down roughly $12 billion in total debt, a hangover from legacy acquisitions and legal settlements, driving toward its target leverage ratio of 2.0x net debt-to-earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) by the end of 2027.

Over the past decade, it has decreased quarterly net debt by 61%.

Major rating agencies (Moody's, S&P, and Fitch) upgraded Teva back to investment-grade status, enabling the company to successfully refinance $4.9 billion of debt on more favorable terms.

The turnaround is not without headwinds. U.S. generic revenues dropped 39% year-over-year to $718 million in the quarter due to intense market competition.

The company is on track to see a slight decline in revenue this year. It also saw earnings per share (EPS) fall 47% year-over-year to $0.20 in the third quarter.

Maintaining growth depends entirely on the continued commercial ramp of its innovative portfolio, supplemented by recent product approvals such as Weltruza to offset the natural drag of legacy commoditized generics.

However, the company appears headed in the right direction and, despite its rise this year, remains a bargain, trading at less than 21 times forward earnings.

James Halley has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3084. CoreWeave credit default swaps hit distress levels as investors sour on AI debt

CoreWeave's credit stress continued to spiral on Thursday amid a broader sell-off in AI-related names.

CoreWeave — a so-called neocloud company — has taken on more than $51 billion of debt and obligations to finance its AI infrastructure build-out. It's now facing a rebellion from stock and bond investors as concerns mount about AI overspending and debt levels.

Yields on CoreWeave debt have continued to climb, with the yield to maturity on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 reaching 15.78%. Bond yields move inversely to prices. Meanwhile, the spread on its five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default, at one point soared above 1,140 basis points. It last stood at nearly 1,038 bps — a 16% week-over-week jump and 164% higher than a year ago.

Credit concerns are spreading into AI equities too. CoreWeave's stock dropped during trading Thursday before recovering and closing 5.94% higher. The moved followed Wednesday's 15.5% nosedive.

Other neocloud stocks were under similar pressure. Nebius also sank before climbing higher to close 1.29% and and Iren ultimately ended the day down less than 1%.

Anxiety about AI overspending is also spreading. For instance, shares of Micron Technology and Advanced Micro Devices sold off following a big sell-off in South Korean chip stocks. Micron closed up 1.87%, while AMD fell 1.5%.

While AI debt worries have been rising steadily, investors have suddenly grown skittish about the underlying finances of companies borrowing heavily to buy chips, build data centers, and rent out computing power.

Coreweave's credit default swaps are one place to see that stress.

Credit default swaps are contracts where buyers pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. The spread widening in CoreWeave's CDS means investors view the debt as riskier.

The market is "basically saying 'no mas' to AI debt without operating cash flow support," the Evercore ISI trading desk said.

A spread level of 1,000 basis points or above is generally a sign of credit distress, said Gavan Nolan, executive director of fixed income pricing at S&P Global Market Intelligence.

No one is questioning whether demand for CoreWeave's services is real, since the company has contracts with all the big hyperscalers, Nolan said. The question is whether the cash flows will ultimately arrive.

"It's obviously heavily negative free cash flow at the moment, and it needs that cash flow to arrive really to justify all the capex that it's spending," Nolan said.

CEO Mike Intrator dismissed the market moves on Wednesday, according to a memo obtained by CNBC.

"When you are building a new type of technology company there will be periods of time when the market misunderstands and acts irrationally," he wrote. "We are confident in our backlog which more than supports all of our debt obligations."

The backlog is over $100 billion, with around two-thirds of it with investment-grade counterparts including Microsoft, Meta, IBM and Caterpillar, Deutsche Bank said in a note Thursday. The firm reiterated its buy-rating on the stock Wednesday, calling the dislocation a buying opportunity.

"The problem is this kind of concern can prove to be a self-fulfilling prophecy, and risks triggering broader issues in other AI-related names," said Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research.

Domino effects may already be happening. Oracle's five-year CDS spread also spiked this week. It was last at 282 basis points, a roughly 10% increase in one week.

"A downgrade from its BBB rating would risk pressuring IG-only investors into forced sales," Reid said.

#3077. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test
