#3001. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#2999. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2997. Yaxel Lendeborg has changed everything for Steph Curry, Warriors this season

This was supposed to be a wasted season for Steph Curry.

The Warriors were injury-riddled and coming off a lackluster summer in which they made no major moves.

It meant the greatest shooter of all time was destined to tread water as he turned 39 years old.

He has resuscitated Curry's career. He has breathed new life into a flailing dynasty. He has given the Warriors their future franchise star.

Lendeborg was a question mark when the Warriors selected him as the No. 11 pick in the 2026 draft.

Can a jack-of-all-trades turn into a star? Can a 24-year-old surpass expectations or has he already peaked? Can a rookie really be NBA ready?

Lendeborg has emphatically answered those questions.

He's averaging 21.5 points (50% shooting from the field, 40% from 3), seven rebounds and 3.9 assists for the Warriors, who have won five games in a row.

Golden State Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg dribbles against the Phoenix Suns during an NBA game at Chase Center in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

The Warriors are good. Curry has a wingman. This is thrilling for anyone who loves basketball.

No one seems to be savoring this more than Curry, whose joy has been on full display amid the Warriors' unexpected rise.

During his 44-point deluge against the Timberwolves on Oct. 28, Curry flashed a lunge pose after sinking a shot from beyond the arc.

After his jab sent Luka Doncic onto his butt and he swished a 3-pointer against the Lakers on Oct. 30, he did the Edvard Munch celebration, putting his hands on his cheeks and opening his mouth in shock.

And after scoring 34 points against the Suns on Monday, he didn't hesitate when asked what has changed for the Warriors this season.

"I mean, Yax," Curry said, flashing a smile.

Golden State Warriors guard Steph Curry warms up before a game against the Los Angeles Lakers in Los Angeles, Wednesday, Oct. 21, 2026. London Entertainment

Curry and Lendeborg are the NBA's second-highest scoring duo (51.5 points per game), behind Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves (61.8). Lendeborg's success has been a life raft for the Warriors, who are without Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody for the first few months of the season and without Kristaps Porzingis indefinitely.

In many ways, Lendeborg has unlocked Curry. He has made his brilliance count. He has lit a new fire under him.

Curry, who missed 27 straight games because of runner's knee last season, is averaging 30 points (46.8% shooting from the field, 42.9% from beyond the arc), 4.2 rebounds and 4.5 assists per game.

He just surpassed Michael Jordan (44) for the most 40-point games in NBA history since turning 30, notching his 45th such performance last week.

Curry is playing as though he were a decade younger. And that's because a guy 14 years younger than him is making all of this meaningful.

It's funny because this really couldn't have worked out better for both parties.

Curry, a two-time MVP and four-time champion, is doing what he loves most: winning.

And the outspoken Lendeborg has found a team that needs him to contribute immediately and encourages his bravado, two things that could've been problematic with other franchises.

Yaxel Lendeborg #1 of the Golden State Warriors drives to the basket against Jalen Green #4 of the Phoenix Suns in San Francisco on Nov. 2, 2026. Getty Images

And neither is being politically correct.

The rookie recently raised eyebrows when he stunningly said he lost all respect for Dončić following their trash talk in the season opener. The Warriors didn't wince.

Instead, Curry, Draymond Green and coach Steve Kerr unapologetically had his back.

Lendeborg landed in the perfect situation. He has a mentor in Green, who's famous for unabashedly speaking his mind. And he has a franchise behind him with a win-now mentality that needs him to shine.

Lendeborg has found his home. He can flourish here. He needs to flourish here.

It's in the form of a rookie who has made it so that instead of treading water he's soaring.

#2996. AI stocks hammered by midterm shock as data center buildout becomes a 'war of attrition'

The Democrats' stunning rout of the Republicans during the midterms this week wasn't just a stinging rebuke of President Donald Trump, but also a potential roadblock to huge plans to build artificial intelligence data centers across the country.

Since the landmark results came in, tech and AI stocks have been jolted by market volatility.

The most valuable tech companies in the U.S. and Europe were hit with a massive sell-off on Thursday as investors got nervous.

Nvidia lost 9.3%, with AMD shedding 17.3%, Microsoft 3.6%, Micron 17.5%, Broadcom 10.4%, Tesla 10.8% and SpaceX 9.1%.

Neoclouds, which rely on their ability to serve AI computing power and are involved in massive data center projects, were also hit particularly hard. Nebius dropped 15%, Iren fell 17% and CoreWeave 10% on Thursday.

By Friday morning, U.S. tech stocks were showing signs of recovery, but the market blip is a warning of potentially rougher roads ahead for the AI boom in America.

"The tumble in tech stocks that took hold in the immediate aftermath of the US midterm elections resumed as investors contemplated what a Democrat-controlled Congress may mean for tech regulation and the overall AI boom," wrote Kyle Rodda, senior financial market analyst at Capital.com in a note on Thursday after the bell.

There were dozens of local ballot measures in 10 states up for a vote on Tuesday that would place restrictions on the construction of data centers in different ways, CNBC reported on Wednesday.

"The regulatory and political risks arise at a time when the proverbial AI trade was already on shakier foundations," said Rodda.

Earnings season had been an "overall disappointment" for the markets, he added, with Magnificent Seven results so far "undershooting growth expectations and flashing warning signs that the pace of AI spending is slowing down."

Now, Rodda wrote, the risk that the investment boom could be slowed further "by measures as potentially disruptive as moratoriums on new data center construction, investors are rushing to the exits on so-called AI trades."

Opposition to data centers in the U.S. is a long-standing issue.

Between January 2024 and May 2026, at least 46 AI data center projects across 20 U.S. states, representing more than $170 billion in announced investment, were blocked, withdrawn or stalled following community opposition, according to research carried out by think tank International Data Center Authority, or IDCA.

The midterms will weigh on investors' minds, Dan Ives, partner at Yorkville Ives & Co., said in a note on Wednesday morning.

"For tech investors the big focus is will these mid-term elections slow the pace of the planned 800 to 1,000 datacenters across the US fueling the AI Revolution…the answer in our view is a clear YES," he wrote.

Democrats taking control, particularly over the Senate, will create some major hurdles for AI over the next two years, Ives added.

The figures floating around on the scale of projected AI infrastructure spending are eye-watering.

Goldman Sachs estimates that AI-related capital spending could total about $7.6 trillion from 2026 through 2031, across computing, data centers and power.

But, heading into the election, AI stocks were already looking shaky on Wall Street.

Chipmakers, neoclouds and other data center-related companies sold off in October on concerns about rising interest rates and overspending. Many hyperscalers have turned cash flow negative as they spend hundreds of billions of dollars on AI infrastructure.

"AI pushback is bipartisan but local, making the buildout pricier rather than stalling it," said Dominique Toublan, head of U.S. credit strategy at Barclays Investment Bank, in a note from Wednesday.

"The AI race shifts from a prisoner's dilemma to a war of attrition: scarce power favors the biggest balance sheets."

By Thursday morning, after speaking to investors over the past 24 hours, Ives said it was a confusing time for Wall Street tech backers trying to "discern the poker game in the Beltway and how this will truly impact the AI Revolution buildout."

"Putting all the pieces together we believe Big Tech capex growth rate now peaks in 2027 vs our previous view that would not happen until 2028/2029," he said.

Chinese AI lab Moonshot turned to Europe for Nvidia Blackwell access amid U.S. China curbs, sources tell CNBC.

CrowdStrike launches AI kill switch that it says can stop internal agents that go rogue.

Meta seeks to raise up to $25 billion in debt sale as AI spending jumps.

Brookfield plans for a $27 billion AI data center at UK power station.

Elon Musk's Tesla and SpaceX lose over $420 billion in combined market cap after midterms.

Blue Origin pushes launch to January 2027 as it races to rebuild damaged launchpad.

Elsewhere in midterm-land, it's not just data centers in the crosshairs.

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, my colleague Tanaya Macheel writes.

The result? It could usher in a more adversarial approach in Washington and push industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner, she says.

#2993. HSA-eligible health plans in 2027: What to know about tax savings vs. higher deductibles

Healthcare affordability was part of the midterm election cost-of-living debate.

It is uncertain how a new Congress might try to address that pain point. Now, as workers choose their 2027 health insurance coverage, millions face a more immediate decision: whether to pick a plan that lets them contribute to a health savings account, or HSA.

The question is whether an HSA-eligible plan with lower premiums and tax benefits is worth the higher deductible and potential out-of-pocket costs.

One noted advantage of an HSA is its tax benefits.

"You have a triple tax advantage," said Kalei Cadinha-Pua'a, vice chair and CEO of Honolulu-based Cadinha & Co., which ranked No. 21 on this year's CNBC Financial Advisor 100 list.

Employer and employee contributions go into the fund before taxes, earnings in the account aren't taxed while they remain in the HSA, and withdrawals for qualified medical expenses are tax-free.

Read coverage of CNBC's 2026 Financial Advisor 100 list, including stories featuring ranked firms and their advisors:

Financial advisors say employees often overlook another potential tax savings. HSA contributions made through an employer's Section 125 cafeteria plan generally aren't subject to Social Security and Medicare taxes.

That can make payroll contributions more tax-efficient than contributing on your own and claiming a deduction later, said certified financial planner and enrolled agent Andy Panko, the founder of Tenon Financial in Metuchen, New Jersey.

Workers should check how their employer processes contributions and ask if they receive the Social Security and Medicare tax break on HSA contributions.

A worker who chooses to contribute to an HSA outside of payroll may be able to deduct eligible contributions on their federal tax return but generally won't get the break on Social Security and Medicare taxes.

HSAs are typically paired with a high-deductible health plan, so consumers generally have to be enrolled in a high-deductible health plan in order to contribute to the HSA.

Such a plan may have lower premiums relative to others available to consumers, but workers can face higher costs on the back end when they need care.

For 2027, the IRS says an HSA-eligible plan must generally have a deductible of at least $1,750 for individual coverage or $3,500 for family coverage. The plan's annual out-of-pocket expenses, excluding premiums, cannot exceed $8,700 for individual coverage or $17,400 for family coverage.

More employers are offering health plans with an HSA option. Across all U.S. health plan sponsors with at least 50 employees, 63% offer a high-deductible plan that qualifies for an HSA, according to a recent Marsh employer survey. More than a third, 37%, of all employees covered by an employer health plan are enrolled in an HSA-eligible plan.

"Employers often offer an HSA plan as a low-cost option, and then because of the higher deductible and the overall cost-sharing structure, these plans are typically costing less for the employer as well as the employee," said Frank Easley, senior vice president of Aon Health Solutions.

An HSA's tax advantages don't by themselves make an HSA-eligible plan the right choice. Workers should consider how the plan's premiums, deductible and out-of-pocket maximum align with expected healthcare needs before enrolling, financial advisors say.

They should also factor in any employer HSA contributions and the cash they have available to pay an unexpected medical bill before they reach their deductible, says Nathan Lochstampfor, CFP, an associate consultant at Harrisburg, Pennsylvania-based Conrad Siegel Investment Advisors, which is ranked No. 45 on CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 for 2026.

If consumers can't afford to pay the deductible of a high-deductible health plan, it may make sense to choose a plan with higher premiums and lower out-of-pocket costs if they need care, he said.

Lochstampfor recommends clients ask themselves: "If I got in a car accident going home today from work, could I afford that deductible? Or would I be better off maybe paying a little bit of higher premiums until I build up those cash reserves?"

HSA contribution limits for 2027 are $4,500 for individuals and $9,000 for families. People age 55 and older can make a $1,000 catch-up contribution.

You can use an HSA to pay for qualified medical expenses now or later. Any money left in the account generally carries over from year to year, and earnings aren't taxed while they remain in the account. HSA balances can also be invested. As of midyear, 11% of HSA accounts held invested assets, according to Devenir, an HSA investment firm.

Ideally, financial advisors say, you keep some of the balance in cash to cover short-term needs and invest the rest for retirement.

"If you have a long time and you can begin to develop a strategy for savings, it can really work and benefit you in the future when you don't have that earned income to pay for your healthcare needs," Cadinha-Pua'a said.

Some HSA owners pay current medical expenses out of pocket and leave money invested in the account so it can grow tax-free. They can use their HSA funds to reimburse themselves years later for those out-of-pocket expenses. Some conditions apply: It must be a qualified medical expense and incurred after the HSA was established, for example.

That strategy can be smart — but it requires good records.

The IRS wants to make sure expenses aren't double counted. Account holders need to keep records showing that any HSA distributions paid or reimbursed qualified medical expenses weren't also claimed as an itemized deduction.

"Be really honest with yourself," Cadinha-Pua'a said. "Are you a good record keeper?"

She recommends making digital copies of receipts, bills and proof of payment, organizing them in an electronic folder and backing up that folder to the cloud. That can help preserve the documentation if a computer fails or the account holder needs to substantiate a reimbursement years later.

#2991. 13 young Eagles worth watching in what's left of a lost season

13 young Eagles worth watching in what's left of a lost season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles aren't even at the halfway point yet.

There are still nine games to go in this miserable 2026 season. And now that the Eagles are sitting at 2-6 with a point differential of -65 it probably feels a little daunting to have to watch this team play nine more times.

Maybe some Eagles fans really will do something else on Sundays — not to mention Thanksgiving and Christmas Eve — but let's be honest: Most of you are still going to watch when the Eagles play.

So other than rooting for a good draft pick, how do you find enjoyment out of a lost season like this? The answer is pretty easy. You get to monitor the development of the young players on the team. And, hopefully, you'll be able to see their progress in the second half of this season.

With that in mind, here are 13 young Eagles who are worth watching the rest of the way:

QB Joe Milton: Maybe the Eagles really do just ride it out with backup quarterback Tanner McKee in place of Jalen Hurts. But there's some definite intrigue with Milton. The Eagles signed him off the Cowboys' practice squad on Oct. 16 and it didn't take long for Milton to supplant Cole Payton as the new backup. The 26-year-old Milton (6-5, 236) has played in just five career games but the former sixth-round pick has plenty of arm talent. His highlight reel from his 2023 season at Tennessee is littered with highlight plays. Milton has a big arm and can elude pressure. He just wasn't very consistent. But in this lost season, if McKee continues to struggle, it would be fun to see Milton get a chance.

QB Cole Payton: The Eagles on Sunday night made an interesting decision. They kept Cole Payton active over offensive lineman Jake Majors but Payton didn't play. My first thought was that Payton would have a role on special teams but he didn't. So perhaps there was an offensive role in mind for Payton that the Eagles didn't get to in their 27-10 loss — or maybe he was just an emergency RB option. Payton is a heck of an athlete and while he's developing as a QB behind the scenes, maybe there's a chance to get him on the field in some other roles.

RB Jaydon Blue: In the loss to the Commanders, Blue played just 8 snaps on offense and 7 snaps on special teams, despite Saquon Barkley being banged up and others missing. Blue 22, was a fifth-round pick out of Texas last year but played in just five games with the Cowboys as a rookie. Blue has an intriguing skillset. He ran a 4.38 at the Combine last year and also showed an ability as a receiving threat at Texas. He's a different kind of back and it would be fun to see him get some burn.

WR Makai Lemon: The first-round pick from USC got off to a slow start in his rookie season but has been getting better and better once the Eagles scrapped the portion of the playbook that had him going backwards. Over the last couple of weeks without DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks, Lemon has played more and has had his two biggest games of the season. Overall, Lemon has 31 catches on 45 targets for 251 yards and a touchdown. Among rookies, Lemon is sixth in receiving yards behind Carnell Tate, Denzel Boston, KC Concepcion, Germie Bernard and Antonio Williams. But Lemon has been coming on stronger recently.

WR Darius Cooper: The UDFA from Tarleton State is still just in his second NFL season. He made the roster again in 2026 and has 11 catches for 175 yards and 4 touchdowns this year. But he has caught just 45.8% of his targets, which is a very low number. Cooper hasn't been a star but he has made some big-time catches and should continue to get some run the rest of the way.

TE Eli Stowers: The second-round pick from Vanderbilt missed the first six games of the season on Injured Reserve with a quad injury. But Stowers made his NFL debut in Week 7 and then made his first NFL catches in Week 8. A couple of those catches came in garbage time but it was still good to see him finish with 3 for 53. When the Eagles drafted Stowers, we knew he was a developmental tight end. The best way to develop is to get him more playing time this season. Stowers is far from a finished product but he has the type of athleticism that's worth developing.

OL Willie Lampkin: The Eagles have had Landon Dickerson back in the starting lineup at left guard the last couple of weeks, but it'll be interesting to see if Dickerson remains with the team through the trade deadline. If not, maybe we see a bit more of Lampkin. The 5-foot-11 offensive lineman started a couple of games at left guard in between Cam Jurgens and Dickerson. Even if Lampkin can't get back on the field as a starter, the Eagles could find some unique ways to use him. He lined up as a fullback on the Saquon Barkley direct-snap touchdown on Sunday night.

OG Micah Morris: The sixth-round pick from Georgia has played just one snap all season so he probably won't get many chances to play this season. But if more injuries hit, Morris could get some chances. He's a really athletic rookie who is worth developing.

C Drew Kendall: The Eagles drafted Kendall out of Boston College in the fifth round last year and have been high on him for a while. We saw that this year when they got him into the starting lineup from Week 2. Kendall has now started five games at center this season and could be the Eagles' long-term fix at the position. The Eagles traded away his predecessor Cam Jurgens earlier this season, so if Kendall plays well the rest of the way, he could keep that job for a while.

OT Markel Bell: The third-round pick from Miami has had some ups and downs this season. Nick Sirianni seemed impressed by Bell's performance against the Cowboys, but Bell didn't have his best game against the Commanders. Lane Johnson started the first three games of the season before he abruptly retired and Bell has now started the last five games. According to PFF, Bell has given up 17 pressures and 5 sacks this season. He has had some good moments in the run game too. Johnson seemed really impressed by Bell in training camp; we'll see if Bell ends up being his long-term replacement.

DT TY Robinson: Robinson played 104 defensive snaps as a rookie fourth-round pick last year. Through eight games this season, Robinson has played just 52 snaps and is firmly behind Jalen Carter, Jordan Davis, Moro Ojomo and Byron Young on the depth chart. Robinson has played just 9% of the snaps, while Young has been on the field for 27%. Robinson had a solid training camp and preseason so it might make sense to start having him eat into Young's snaps.

LB Jihaad Campbell: This is a tricky one because Campbell began the season as a full-time starter next to Zack Baun and then the Eagles started up a rotation between Campbell and Jeremiah Trotter Jr. While it looks like Trotter might simply be the best option right now, Campbell was a first-round pick and has a ton of athletic ability. Campbell hasn't lived up to expectations but he's in Year 2 as a first-round pick. The only way for him to get better is to continue to play.

S Drew Mukuba: Mukuba is in a similar position as Campbell but there's no one like Trotter pushing him. The second-year, second-round pick from Texas has had a mostly disappointing season as a full-time starter in 2026. But he did have a good game against the Commanders with a forced fumble and an interception that was negated by a penalty. Through eight games, Mukuba has played 556 of 558 snaps. We might learn a lot about him over the final nine games of the season.

#2989. Nvidia's Huang is asked to brief Congress on reported Chinese spy relationship

Three House Republican committee leaders asked Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang to sit for a closed-door briefing on his relationship with a tech executive reportedly identified as a Chinese intelligence asset.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information," the GOP lawmakers wrote in a letter to Huang unveiled Thursday, using the acronym for the People's Republic of China.

They are seeking information on Huang's interactions with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's "security protocols against foreign adversary espionage," they wrote in the brief message, which is dated Wednesday.

The letter does not specify which reports it is referring to. But The New York Times reported last week that U.S. intelligence found Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen determined to be funneling information to Beijing, had "developed a close relationship" with Jensen Huang.

President Donald Trump, who himself has forged close ties with Jensen Huang as he champions the U.S. artificial intelligence industry, was briefed about a year ago on those intelligence findings, the Times reported.

Trump "chose not to do anything with the information," according to the newspaper, which cited interviews with people briefed on the surveillance and other sources.

The letter to Huang was signed by House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast, R-Fla., Select Committee on China Chairman John Moolenaar, R-Mich., and House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence Chairman Rick Crawford, R-Ark.

Nvidia did not directly respond when asked by CNBC to comment on the letter and confirm whether Huang would attend the lawmakers' proposed briefing.

Nvidia instead provided a statement that largely mirrored the one it previously sent the Times, in which a spokesperson suggested Jensen Huang's interactions with Alice Huang were no different from his meetings with other executive clients.

"Our regional cloud customers receive ordinary business information — not sensitive NVIDIA trade secrets—and zero information relevant to national security," the spokesperson said in the statement.

"Meetings with customers are part of a CEO's job; Jensen talks with thousands of customer executives every year," the spokesperson said. "We're confident that if the U.S. government believes that any person is a threat to American industry, it would let us know."

A spokeswoman for Mast did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for additional information. Megaspeed did not immediately respond to an email seeking comment.

The lawmakers invited the Nvidia CEO to appear for an in-person joint briefing on Nov. 18. Nvidia is scheduled to report its third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17.

"China's access to that sensitive U.S. tech is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress," the committee leaders wrote. "It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which U.S. national security has been placed at risk."

#2986. Bronny James headed to G League after dismal start to Lakers season

Bronny James is headed east to California's Coachella Valley, as the Lakers have assigned the third-year player to its G League affiliate.

The team announced the move Thursday, and the 22-year-old, along with teammate Cameron Carr, will suit up for the Coachella Valley Lakers, which begin their season on Friday. This follows the Los Angeles Lakers' 116-103 win over the Sacramento Kings on Wednesday.

James barely factored into the game, logging a single minute. Early on, he hasn't been able to crack regular minutes this season.

Through nine games, James has had cameos in five contests with averages of 2.4 minutes and 0.8 points per game. His most recent productive effort came against the Heat when he chipped in two points and a block in a single minute of play.

Lakers news: Is this the best version of Lakers star Luka Dončić we've ever seen?

Bronny James has seen his minutes decrease drastically from the previous season, wherein he logged 6.7 minutes per game over 27 games in his rookie campaign and 8.9 minutes per game over 42 games the following season, 2025-2026.

James' father, legendary NBA star LeBron James, is no longer with the Lakers. The 41-year-old is playing for the Philadelphia 76ers on a two-year deal with a player option.

For the Lakers, the early returns for the roster have been positive. The team is 7-2 and is atop the Pacific Division thanks to inspired play from star Luka Dončić. With eight players averaging 23 minutes or more per game, it's clear James has less opportunity to crack the rotation.

The Coachella Valley Lakers open play at Acrisure Arena on Friday, Nov. 6 vs. the Salt Lake City Stars. Whether the game is a precursor to a brief stint or a more prolonged development period remains to be seen.

#2982. Neocloud IREN says $4 billion in annualized revenue being delayed due to customer request

Following a 26% post-election plunge in its stock price, neocloud IREN said in its earnings report on Thursday that over $4 billion in annual recurring revenue projected to land in 2026 has been pushed out to at least January.

IREN said the move, which could extend payment to February, is due to "a customer-requested change and supplier timing."

On the company's earnings call, IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts said Microsoft is the customer.

"They made a request to reconfigure and upgrade some of the network infrastructure and the purpose of that was really to enable the 200 megawatts of capacity to service these latest-generation frontier models," Roberts said.

Shares of IREN rose in extended trading, but that comes after a 55% drop over the past month.

The company said revenue climbed 66% from the prior quarter to $228 million, beating the $221 million average analyst estimate, according to LSEG. AI Cloud revenue more than doubled to $200 million. IREN gets the rest of its revenue from bitcoin mining, which was its primary business before the company pivoted to AI.

IREN's net loss narrowed to $263 million from $684 million the prior period. Year-ago figures weren't included in the earnings release.

The company is being closely watched by investors as the AI trade shows signs of unwinding, particularly following the Democratic sweep in Tuesday's midterm elections. Concerns are mounting that cash-burning companies reliant on debt are going to have a hard time meeting their obligations as interest rates rise and lenders back out of the market. The Nasdaq had its worst day on Thursday since April 2025.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the call.

CoreWeave, which is set to report earnings on Tuesday, has plunged 60% in the past month. Nebius, which reports next Thursday, is down 48% over that stretch.

IREN said revenue backlog increased 22% from the prior period to $20.3 billion, with "approximately two-thirds with investment-grade counterparties." The $4 billion ARR target is fully contracted and funded, the company said.

"There is a disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level," Roberts told CNBC's Seema Mody.

Still, Iren said it planed to shell out $15 billion in capital expenditures the rest of this fiscal year, including $9 billion in cash and committed financing, and $6 billion in "target" financing, which is "subject to market conditions."

The neocloud said that prices for its compute continue to rise and that it expanded an existing contract with a leading frontier AI lab. It said it has contracts with Nvidia, Microsoft and 13 other customers.

Roberts said that some data center construction in the coming year would be backed by Nvidia and Microsoft, but about a third would be to companies that are willing to offer large prepayments for the capacity.

"We just had a customer agree to fund 90% of the GPU capex," Roberts said, referring to graphics processing units.

IREN said that community opposition can be addressed by tapping local suppliers, using water efficiently and creating jobs.

"I think whether it's Texas, whether it's Australia, whether it's Spain, everywhere, all we're seeing is people wanting to ensure that data centers pay their own way and do the right thing, and it it feels completely reasonable," Roberts said.

IREN said it deliberately hadn't yet bought all of the GPUs it needs for next year to reduce risk.

—CNBC's Isabel O'Brien contributed to this report.

#2980. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."

#2976. A long-term Elon Musk backer is cashing out of Tesla — and cutting its SpaceX exposure

A key early investor has sold the last of its Tesla shares and sharply reduced its SpaceX holding, taking profits from two of its most successful long-term bets.

Scottish Mortgage Investment Trust, a London-listed closed-end fund managed by Baillie Gifford, said Friday that SpaceX represented 11.1% of its total assets as of Wednesday, down from 15% at Sept. 30.

The position helped drive Scottish Mortgage's 31.5% net asset value return in the six months to the end of September. In its half-year results statement, Scottish Mortgage also said it sold its remaining Tesla stake in April, ending an investment that dated back to 2013.

Scottish Mortgage first invested in SpaceX in December 2018, when Elon Musk's rocket company had a reported valuation of about $31 billion, and committed roughly $200 million through 2021.

The investment surged in value as SpaceX expanded its launch business and Starlink satellite network. By the time of its blockbuster IPO in June, SpaceX represented roughly a quarter of Scottish Mortgage's portfolio by value.

The investment trust has since sold shares as post-IPO lock-up restrictions have lifted in stages, though it said its conviction in SpaceX remains high.

"The decision to reduce the position following its flotation, while retaining a substantial investment, demonstrates the importance of balancing conviction with disciplined portfolio management," Scottish Mortgage said.

SpaceX was a major contributor to Scottish Mortgage's performance during the period, adding 8 percentage points to returns.

The trust's net asset value, or NAV, delivered a total return of 31.5% in the six months through Sept. 30, compared with a 16% return for the FTSE All-World Index. Its share price returned 33.8%.

Proceeds from the SpaceX sales also helped Scottish Mortgage repay borrowings and reduce gearing.

The Tesla sale closes another highly profitable bet for Scottish Mortgage, which invests globally in a mix of public and private high-growth companies, typically with a longer-term investment view.

In a portfolio update in July, the fund said it had invested around $470 million in Tesla, and generated approximately $6.2 billion in realized profit over 13 years.

"Since we first invested in 2013, it has returned 13 times the capital invested, but we felt the share price had come to reflect much of its promise in autonomy and robotics," Scottish Mortgage said on Friday.

The trust also pointed to intensifying competition in Tesla's core automotive business, particularly in China.

Shares in SpaceX and Tesla fell about 9% and 11%, respectively, on Thursday, taking their combined market value more than $420 billion below Tuesday’s close. Investors fear Elon Musk could face new congressional probes after the U.S. midterms, potentially exposing his companies to tighter regulation.

Scottish Mortgage is one of several U.K. investment trusts — comparable to closed-end funds in the U.S. — that were early backers of SpaceX.

Another Baillie Gifford fund, Edinburgh Worldwide Investment Trust, was the focus of a bitter activist campaign by Boaz Weinstein's hedge fund Saba Capital in 2025, with its SpaceX holding becoming a key flashpoint in the long-running battle.

#2974. Could CRISPR cure rare diseases before birth? Scientists are getting closer

One of the keys to curing a rare disease is treating patients early. But what if you treated them before they were born?

Over the past decade, several researchers have pursued this provocative idea. Now, they are starting to make meaningful progress in fetal gene editing, an approach that holds the potential to stop genetic disorders before it's too late.

"If you really want to prevent the worst ravages of the disease, you can't wait until they're born," said Kiran Musunuru, a gene editing expert at the University of Pennsylvania. "You've got to go in utero."

The futuristic technique is benefiting from new advances in prenatal diagnostics and CRISPR, the Nobel Prize-winning DNA editing tool. Other scientists pursuing the idea include David Stitelman at the Yale School of Medicine, Tippi MacKenzie at University of California, San Francisco, and Aijun Wang, a biomedical engineer at UC Davis.

For more stories like this, sign up for the CNBC Cures newsletter.

Musunuru made headlines is 2025 when he helped develop the world's first personalized gene editing treatment for an infant with a rare genetic disorder. But long before that, he began studying the potential for fetal gene editing with William Peranteau, a co-director of the Center for Fetal Research at the Children's Hospital of Philadelphia.

Progress had been slow. But the pair have discovered something they were not expecting.

After babies are born, their bodies' blood vessels erect barriers to help them breathe on their own. That has made it difficult to deliver gene-editing treatments to organs beyond the liver. As a result, scientists are limited in the types of diseases that they can go after.

But before babies are born, it's a different story. To their surprise, Musunuru and Peranteau found that CRISPR's molecular scissors can reach the heart and other hard-to-reach muscles of unborn animals. Their discovery could open the door to fixing genetic muscle disorders like Duchenne muscular dystrophy before the damage is done.

At Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, Peranteau is part of a team that has performed surgeries on fetuses with birth defects like spina bifida, where the spinal cord is exposed during birth. The mother goes under anesthesia while the surgeon opens up the uterus and operates on the fetus to correct the birth defect. Afterward, the unborn baby continues to develop until ready to be born.

Fetal gene editing would be less invasive than that, Peranteau said. It would be similar to how doctors routinely treat unborn babies diagnosed with fetal anemia by performing a blood transfusion through the umbilical vein.

Musunuru and Peranteau have seen promising early results in animals. But they are at least three years away from trying the approach in humans and careful to temper expectations, worrying that they might give families false hope. While the scientists believe they could treat heart and muscle disorders, getting past the blood-brain barrier still remains difficult. That means, for now, neurological conditions are off the table.

The scientists plan to make edits to what are called somatic cells. They want to steer clear of editing reproductive cells, an approach that has become one of science's biggest controversies. To many researchers, editing those cells crosses an ethical line because it can affect future generations and lead to so-called "designer babies" whose parents could modify their genes to make their descendants taller or faster. After a scientist revealed in 2018 that he had edited embryos of twin girls to protect them from HIV, a Chinese court sentenced him to three years in prison. Despite the controversy, some researchers are studying DNA editing of embryos.

Editing the DNA of a fetus has not been controversial. And new breakthroughs are bringing it closer to reality. Scientists can deliver CRISPR components by infusing trillions of microscopic fat bubbles known as lipid nanoparticles into a patient's bloodstream. The tiny containers – which hold the tools to fix DNA misspellings and guide them to the right place – are considered safer than the older method of ferrying the drug through the body inside the shell of an inactivated virus.

Meanwhile, diagnostic companies are offering "non-invasive prenatal testing," promising to identify whether a fetus is at risk of a genetic disease by testing the mother's blood. The approach is seen as less risky than screening a fetus for conditions like Down syndrome by guiding a thin, hollow needle through the mother's abdomen, known as amniocentesis.

"People used to ask `Can you even diagnose any of these things before birth?'" said Stitelman, the Yale professor who has studied fetal gene editing for over a decade. "But the technology to make these diagnoses is improving. It seems less far-fetched now than it did when I started."

For Musunuru and Peranteau, the hardest question ahead may be which diseases to tackle first. They are looking at disorders that start before birth and do not have effective treatments. They want to focus on conditions "where there's a good scientific and biological rationale to doing it before birth," Peranteau said.

"I want to be efficient and develop these therapies as quickly as possible," Peranteau said. "But more importantly, we need to make sure we're doing it in a safe and ethically responsible way."

CNBC Cures is underwritten by Alexion, AstraZeneca Rare Disease and BridgeBio.

#2973. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

#2972. Molly Miller Goes Viral For Sideline Outfit As Fans Discover Arizona State Coach’s Remarkable Basketball Success

Molly Miller is turning heads both on and off the basketball court. The Arizona State women's basketball coach has gone viral after stepping onto the sidelines in a stylish black outfit, leaving fans asking where they could buy her top. But there's much more to the 41-year-old than her game-day wardrobe. Miller has become one of college basketball's most talked-about coaches after transforming a struggling Arizona State program into an NCAA Tournament contender in just one season. Now, with a new contract and a reloaded roster, she's giving fans plenty of reasons to pay attention.

A post shared by Sun Devil Women's Basketball (@sundevilwbb)

Miller made an impression during Arizona State's season opener against Jackson State on November 2, wearing an all-black ensemble that quickly attracted attention online. The coach paired a fitted black top with matching trousers, keeping her look sleek and polished as she directed her players from the sidelines. On November 4, the Sun Devils women's basketball account shared photos of Miller with the caption, "Stepping into Year 2 in style."

The post sparked enthusiastic reactions from fans who were just as interested in her wardrobe as they were in the team's performance.

"Ok but where is the top from???" one person asked.

"Molly is the best," another wrote, while a third commented, "If you look good you play good it's simple."

According to The Spun, Miller's fashion choices have become a talking point among basketball followers, adding to her growing popularity on social media. Her outfit wasn't the only thing worth celebrating that night. Arizona State opened its season with an impressive 84-41 victory over Jackson State, giving Miller a winning start to her second year in charge.

Thomas Fernandez / Image of Sport Photos/Newscom/MEGA

Long before she became a viral sensation, Miller had already built an impressive reputation in women's college basketball. Born in Springfield, Missouri, she played basketball at Drury University, where she later began her coaching career. Miller spent six seasons leading Drury's women's basketball program, compiling 180 victories and establishing herself as one of the most successful coaches in Division II. Her success eventually brought her to Grand Canyon University in 2020.

During five seasons with the Antelopes, Miller recorded a 117-38 record and transformed the program into a conference powerhouse. Her final season at Grand Canyon was particularly memorable. The team finished 32-3, went undefeated in Western Athletic Conference play with a 16-0 record, and captured both the regular-season and conference tournament championships. The achievement secured Grand Canyon's first NCAA Tournament appearance in women's basketball. Arizona State took notice, hiring Miller in March 2025 to revive its struggling program.

Caitlin Clark made WNBA history after signing a major new endorsement deal that marked another milestone in her basketball career.

LeBron James credited standout players for the growing popularity of women's NCAA basketball as the sport continued attracting national attention.

Paige Bueckers celebrated a remarkable week after winning an NCAA championship and becoming the WNBA's No. 1 draft pick.

Ice Cube made headlines after offering Clark a lucrative deal to join his BIG3 basketball league.

LSU women's basketball players opened up about the attention they received after winning the national championship.

Angel Reese fired back at NC State's head coach following LSU's March Madness victory.

Thomas Fernandez / Image of Sport Photos/Newscom/MEGA

When Miller arrived in Tempe, Arizona State was coming off a disappointing 10-22 season. The Sun Devils had also gone several years without an NCAA Tournament appearance, leaving the new coach with plenty of work to do. Miller wasted little time changing the team's fortunes. In her first season, Arizona State finished 24-11, setting a school record for the most victories by a first-year head coach. The Sun Devils also opened the campaign with 15 consecutive wins and returned to the NCAA Tournament for the first time since 2019. Although their tournament run ended with a First Four loss to Virginia, the turnaround represented a significant achievement for a program that had struggled to find consistency.

According to Arizona State Athletics, the team also set a program attendance record during the 2025-26 season, drawing 61,695 fans. Miller's coaching record placed her among the winningest active women's college basketball coaches by career winning percentage. By the end of her first season at Arizona State, she had accumulated 321 career victories against just 66 losses across her collegiate coaching career.

Thomas Fernandez/Image of Sport/Newscom/MEGA

Miller isn't relying solely on last season's success to keep Arizona State competitive. Ahead of the 2026-27 campaign, the coach and her staff worked to strengthen the roster through the transfer portal. According to Arizona State Athletics, the Sun Devils added Ayanna-Sarai Darrington, Rashunda Jones, Averie Lower, Ella Ryan, and Ruby Whitehorn. The recruiting effort attracted national attention, with ESPN recognizing Arizona State's transfer class as one of the strongest in the country.

The new additions are expected to help Miller build on the momentum established during her first season. Her team's opening performance against Jackson State offered an early glimpse of what the retooled roster could accomplish. Despite falling behind 11-2 in the opening minutes, Arizona State recovered to secure a commanding 84-41 victory. Heloisa Carrera led the Sun Devils with 15 points and nine rebounds. For Miller, the result provided an encouraging start as she attempts to establish Arizona State as a consistent contender in women's college basketball.

Thomas Fernandez/Image of Sport/Newscom/MEGA

Arizona State has already made it clear that it wants Miller leading its women's basketball program for years to come. In August, the university announced a contract extension keeping the coach in Tempe through 2031. Athletics Director Graham Rossini praised her immediate influence on the program.

"Molly's impact has been immediate, creating real momentum and putting Sun Devil Women's Basketball in the national spotlight," Rossini said in the university's announcement.

He added that the extension reflected confidence in Miller's leadership and her ability to build a team capable of competing for championships. Miller also expressed her appreciation for the university's support.

"My family and staff are incredibly grateful for the opportunity to continue building at Arizona State," she said.

The coach, who shares two children with her husband, Derek, has spoken enthusiastically about her future with the Sun Devils. With her team winning games, her coaching reputation growing, and her sideline style attracting a new audience, Miller is entering her second Arizona State season with considerably more attention than when she arrived.

#2971. Outside groups urge congressional Dems to target corporate interests over Trump

As Democrats prepare to take back the gavels in key investigative committees in the next Congress, outside groups are urging them to use their oversight resources to focus more on the cost of living and corporate actors than on President Donald Trump.

Such an approach could resonate more deeply with voters, according to several groups and individuals who have been drafting oversight plans and advising members of Congress. Democrats are expected to launch myriad investigations into companies that have done business with the Trump administration in the technology, cryptocurrency and prediction market industries when they assume power in January.

"We were pretty worried that we were going to repeat the same mistake of 2019, where Democrats came with a big new majority ... and they spent a year-plus on impeachment," Alex Jacquez, a senior vice president of policy, advocacy and research at Groundwork Collaborative, a progressive think tank, told CNBC on Thursday.

"The issue never really resonated for people. Donald Trump was more popular the day that impeachment ended in the Senate than he was when it started in the House," he said.

Several Democratic-aligned groups and strategists expressed similar sentiments to CNBC. They're urging Democrats — who won sizable majorities in both chambers and have an axe to grind against the Trump administration — to show restraint. They should avoid the mistake of spending too much time trying to remove the president from office and stay focused on affordability, Jacquez and others said.

"Voters don't want us to investigate just for the sake of political vendettas. It's not healthy for us as a democracy. It plays into a lot of what Trump has done himself," said Lucas Holtz, a senior political advisor at Third Way, a center-left think tank, which he said has been in close contact with Democratic leaders on oversight and other aspects of their congressional agenda. "Democrats hopefully have learned lessons from Trump and will responsibly investigate in a way that connects back to cost of living."

That strategy is supported by recent polling conducted by Groundwork and polling firm Hart Research that was shared exclusively with CNBC.

It found that 2 in 3 voters think investigations of corporate greed should be a high priority, more than the 58% who believe Congress should prioritize Trump's personal corruption.

And in focus groups conducted by the think tank, Jacquez said there was palpable anger aimed at CEOs and corporate leaders who voters feel have ripped them off.

"A lot of people were pessimistic … that oversight would result in anything, and so they want restitution. They want CEOs to go to jail. They want people to resign. They want prices to be lowered," Jacquez said.

"In the focus groups in particular, I was pretty shocked at how much jail time came up for CEOs," he added.

Whether Democrats heed that advice remains to be seen.

House and Senate leaders are rolling out the foundations of their legislative and investigative agendas, and some presumptive chairs of key committees have flicked at who and what they'll target. They've so far laid out a sprawling hit list, which includes Trump, his family, Cabinet officials, big banks and tech companies.

For one, Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon is expected to lead the Senate Finance Committee and has already set the table for an ambitious oversight agenda.

A person familiar with the matter told CNBC on Thursday that the committee would probe JPMorgan Chase's banking relationship with the late sex offender Jeffrey Epstein, and in particular decisions made by Mary Callahan Erdoes, the CEO of JPMorgan's asset and wealth management division. The person spoke on condition of anonymity because the probe of the nation's biggest bank has not been announced.

The news comes a day after Wyden said in a statement that the panel's top priorities "will be affordability for us, accountability for them."

Wyden said the committee would double down on its efforts to make sure billionaires pay their fair share in taxes, address the "corporate chokehold on our nation's health care system" and reassert congressional authority over trade. He also vowed to target the "special interests" that had cozied up to Trump in the past two years.

Presumptive House Judiciary Committee Chair Jamie Raskin, D-Md., also listed Epstein as a potential source for probes, specifically the Department of Justice's alleged efforts to cover up his human trafficking operation. Raskin also said the committee could probe Trump's $1.8 billion "lawfare fund"; the president's attempt to pardon himself, his family and their businesses; and violations of the emoluments clause by Trump and his Cabinet, which prohibits federal officials from accepting gifts from foreign states.

Another person familiar with Democrats' oversight plans, who was not authorized to speak publicly, told CNBC the strategy is to aggressively target business while also taking a more traditional watchdog role of the executive branch.

Any business that has affiliations with the Trump administration could come into the crosshairs, the person said.

Democrats believe they have a mandate after the election to conduct aggressive investigations into industries like tech, prediction markets and cryptocurrency — all of which have kowtowed to or benefited from the Trump administration over the past two years, the person said. Indeed, the CNBC Voter Poll showed voters did respond resoundingly to Democrats' anti-corruption and affordability message that put tech leaders in the spotlight. The poll was a collaborative effort across several news organizations that drew on about 100,000 interviews conducted by phone, online and in person at polling locations with a margin of error of 0.6%.

Tech companies have publicly embraced the Trump administration, attending and donating to the president's inauguration and, in some cases, giving gifts to the president. The White House has been hands off with artificial intelligence while encouraging the buildout of data centers — a massive infrastructure gambit that is now in jeopardy after data centers proved a loser at the ballot box.

Cryptocurrency and prediction markets, two relatively new players, are also going to be under the microscope, the person said. The crypto investigations will be led by Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the incoming chair of the Senate Banking Committee and a known critic of the industry that has dumped millions of dollars into electoral politics.

Another wrinkle for crypto is the return of Sen.-elect Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, who lost his seat in 2024 to Sen. Bernie Moreno, R-Ohio, after crypto spent millions to have him defeated. Brown was elected Tuesday to Ohio's other Senate seat.

The Trump family has also been intertwined with prediction market companies while making billions on crypto ventures.

The second person said the oversight strategy will both address the perception that companies hold undue influence with the government and benefit from it and will deter the companies from pursuing corrupt behavior or rolling over for the administration.

Democrats expect companies will try to do everything in their power to cooperate, given that the election results were so heavily in their favor, the person said. Helping their cause is that companies cannot avoid congressional subpoenas, and a company itself cannot be pardoned.

The oversight itself will also serve as a means to an end, the person said, publicly surfacing information that could later materially impact a company.

Former Federal Trade Commission Chair Lina Khan echoed that point in a post to X on Thursday.

"Why should Congressional Democrats investigate corporate America if their mandate is to deliver real economic change? Because investigations are a key catalyst for securing reform and legislation," Khan said.

She highlighted the work of the House Judiciary Committee during Trump's first term, which she said conducted an "18-month investigation into Amazon, Apple, Facebook, and Google, securing key evidence of how their business practices were undermining competition and harming people, setting the basis for both lawsuits and proposed legislation."

#2970. How Tanner McKee found out he was benched and what he's doing about it

How Tanner McKee found out he was benched and what he's doing about it originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Nick Sirianni was asked point-blank on Monday if a quarterback change was on the table and he shot it down emphatically. The Eagles were sticking with Tanner McKee, Sirianni insisted.

"Tanner's our guy," Sirianni said on Monday afternoon. "We're not making any changes there.

The next day McKee got the call from Sirianni.

Despite what the head coach said to the world the day before, he was making a quarterback switch. McKee has been benched and Joe Milton, who arrived less than three weeks ago, will start in Week 9 against the Giants. The news broke on Wednesday.

"That's one thing I appreciate about (the team)," McKee said on Thursday. "They've been very upfront about things. I know what he said in the media on Monday, the day before. But he said he wanted to communicate to the guys before he says anything to the media. He doesn't want us finding out in the media, which I appreciate."

McKee, 26, said he isn't on social media, so he didn't know about Sirianni's declaration on Monday until after he found out he was demoted. So at least there's that.

Jalen Hurts began the year as the Eagles' starting quarterback but has been out since the Panthers game with blood clots that are claiming his season. McKee made the last three starts and the Eagles lost all of them. While McKee isn't the only reason they lost, he also wasn't able to do enough to win.

Over the last three weeks, McKee completed 63.2% of his passes for 638 yards with 2 touchdowns and 2 interceptions. He had a passer rating of 76.6 as the Eagles lost to the Panthers, Cowboys and Commanders. And there's no doubt that McKee struggled on Sunday in Washington, especially with his pocket awareness.

"I think, watching the film, different decisions, different throws," McKee said. "Obviously, as a quarterback, have to take accountability for things like that. I think you have to go back and review everything that was done even throughout the week. I thought I had a great process. Thought I felt prepared. I don't have any regrets there. I think you have to go back and be like, 'What could I have done there?' And take that approach as well."

The Eagles initially drafted McKee in the sixth round out of Stanford back in 2023 and groomed him to be their backup quarterback. That was the job he held all of last season coming off a Super Bowl parade. This offseason, though, the Eagles brought in veteran Andy Dalton to compete for that back job and Dalton won. But Dalton was demoted after throwing an interception against the Bears in Week 3 and was eventually released.

It's definitely fair to question how the Eagles have handled the quarterback position all year. McKee went from competing for the backup job to being the 3, then the 2, then the 1 and is now the 2 again.

And he's not resigned to staying in that spot.

"This is the NFL. Competition is always open," McKee said. "I'll keep our conversation private. I don't know how much [Sirianni has] been open to the media about our conversation. But there's always competition. This is the NFL. So you gotta go out, you've got to perform every week. I'm going to approach it as a competition and be ready in case my number is called. Whether that's an injury or my number is just called."

It's not like McKee got to play with a full deck over the last three weeks. He was without DeVonta Smith and Dontavyion Wicks for the last two of those losses and Saquon Barkley hasn't been healthy either. Not to mention, the offensive line hasn't played up to its standard all season.

But when McKee was asked on Thursday why he wasn't given the benefit of the doubt with all the injuries, he made no excuses.

"We have great players and we had great players on the field," McKee said. "The expectation is to go out and win and unfortunately, I fell short doing that. And I gotta be better and make better plays."

Because Milton arrived less than three weeks ago, McKee has been helping him as much as possible this week. Milton missed all of the install during training camp and has had to catch up quickly before his first NFL start on Sunday.

Even though Milton took his job, McKee has been more than willing to help him this week.

"In sports, you gotta have a short memory," McKee said. "I'm still a Philadelphia Eagle. I want the team to perform so anything I can do to help the team, whether that's helping Joe or Jalen or whoever it is, that's my job, that's my role and that's what I'm going to do."

As a 2023 draft pick, McKee is in the final year of his rookie contract with the Eagles. Based on the way this season has gone, there seems to be a pretty good chance he won't be back with the team in 2027.

But McKee isn't trying to rush that fate. McKee was asked on Thursday if he wants to still be here the rest of this season or if he'd prefer a clean break.

"I love it here," he said. "It's a great place. I have a lot of confidence in the coaches, in our scheme, Sean (Mannion). And so I'm happy to be here. I love being here. And I love to compete. Like we said, in the NFL, things are going to go up and down. It's going to be a roller coaster. But you have to love that as a competitor and you have to embrace that. That's my approach. That's what I'm going to do."

#2969. Preview: Klay returns to Chase Center with new look Heat

It's a great time for sports fans to be in the Bay Area, Dub Nation! The Valkyries won their first WNBA title on Halloween night at Chase Center and paraded the trophy down the Embarcadero yesterday. Their older siblings the Golden State Warriors have won five straight. And tonight, the building that just hosted a championship celebration hosts the Miami Heat and a quasi homecoming for a guy who earned a few of those parades himself. Wassup, Klay Thompson!

The Warriors remember the last time Miami came to Chase. Jimmy Butler went down with a torn ACL that night, and he's still out tonight, along with Kristaps Porzingis, Moses Moody and Gui Santos.

Yesterday while San Francisco was sweeping violet confetti off the Embarcadero, Klay Thompson was in Inglewood remembering exactly who he is. He dropped 30 on 8-of-17 from deep to cap a 20-point comeback over the Clippers. Down two in overtime, Giannis grabbed a defensive rebound with 3.8 seconds left, Miami called timeout, and on the ensuing inbound Giannis found Klay open in the left corner off a Bam screen for the walk-off three. Per a stat making the rounds, it was the first game-winning three of Klay's career in the final five seconds of the fourth quarter or OT, regular season and playoffs. Klay's got four rings, 37 points in a quarter, Game 6 in OKC, and yet the walk-off three was the one trinket missing from the shelf.

The Warriors he's coming back to look nothing like the team that started 0-3. They've since beaten the Nuggets, Timberwolves, Lakers, Clippers and Suns, including the Clippers win with both Steph and Draymond sitting out, and Monday's win over Phoenix came after trailing by 17.

The South Beach super team rolls out Davion Mitchell, Thompson, Andrew Wiggins, Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo. Wiggins also won a title with the Warriors back in 2022 and got into the parade action; he joins Klay on the walk to the familiar arena.

Miami's journey so far hasn't been smooth. Miami lost its first four before winning three of the next four, and in that Clippers game everybody not named Klay went 2-of-23 from deep.

Through eight games, Miami ranks dead last in the league in opponent threes made (17.1 per game) and opponent three-point percentage (40.5 percent), which is an engraved invitation for a Steph Curry who's averaging 30 a night. The flip side is a Golden State frontcourt that starts Draymond at center with rookie Yaxel Lendeborg at forward, staring down a two-time MVP and a Bam who just set season highs with 35 points and 10 rebounds. Miami's been getting nearly 20 points a night at the free throw line, so Lendeborg has to wall up Giannis without fouling. When the Warriors shoot, he has to find Giannis and put a body on him before the Greek Freak can turn a long rebound into a one-man fast break. Then there's the Steve Kerr and Erik Spoelstra subplot, two guys who drew up gold-medal sets on the same whiteboard in Paris now trying to burn each other's playbooks.

The schedule does hand Golden State an edge. The Warriors had two days off, while the Heat are on the second night of a back-to-back and their third game in four nights, and Klay's status is still uncertain. If he plays, Chase will rain love on him during introductions, and then 18,000 people will spend the next two hours praying he left his new walk-off habit in Inglewood.
