#2968. 'The whole landscape changed': Gaming CEOs see new opportunities post-midterms

Midterm election results have created new openings for U.S. gambling expansion, while raising the prospect of greater political and legal pressure on prediction markets, according to gaming industry executives.

"The whole landscape changed," said Craig Billings, CEO of Wynn Resorts, during the company's earnings call Thursday.

Wynn has been closely watchng developments in the Middle East as it looks to open the United Arab Emirates' first legal casino in 2027. Billings said he suspects the election will have an impact on the "likely trajectory of the conflict in the region."

Closer to home, Billings sees opportunities in Texas. And he's not the only one. On the heels of reporting record third-quarter revenue and raising guidance for its brick-and-mortar business, Penn Entertainment CEO Jay Snowden called out specific opportunities made possible by state leadership changes.

Election Day was "a very good day overall for the industry, and certainly for Penn," Snowden said on Thursday's earnings call.

Texas, in particular, could be a jackpot for the casino industry. In a massive upset, Democrat Vikki Goodwin defeated longtime Lt. Gov. Dan Patrick, who has been persistent in blocking gambling expansion in the Lone Star State.

"Texas is a fascinating market," Billings said, explaining how many consumers travel to Oklahoma to gamble at tribal casions. "So it's kind of a stealth market that is giant."

Penn has been building a position in Texas for years, Snowden said. It operates two of the state's three Class 1 thoroughbred racetracks and has established relationships with local leaders.

Penn plans to spend more time in Texas and, if lawmakers move toward legalization, Snowden said he expects the company is “positioned as well, if not better than really anybody in the sector.”

Las Vegas Sands also has invested heavily for many years to get casino gambling legalized in Texas.

"When Las Vegas Sands thinks about Texas and the Metroplex, it's really about an asset that will be both for business and future tourism," Sands Chairman and CEO Patrick Dumont told CNBC in an interview in October in Macao.

Dumont recently acquired the controlling stake in the Dallas Mavericks from his mother-in-law Miriam Adelson. He said there is the potential for a new Mavericks arena to incorporate a future casino.

"Whatever Las Vegas Sands can do in the future, that will be its own location, its own site and its own timeline," he said.

Houston Rockets owner Tilman Fertitta, currently serving as U.S. ambassador to Italy, owns Golden Nugget casinos, is Wynn Resorts' largest investor and is buying Caesars to take it private. Following a CNBC interview in Italy, he said, "Of course I want gaming in Texas!"

A new market would draw national sportsbook operators that would likely include DraftKings, Flutter-owned FanDuel, Caesars, Fanatics, and BetMGM, which is co-owned by MGM and Entain, among others.

Though the Texas' state election removed a major political obstacle, it didn't produce a deal. Legalizing casino gambling or sports betting would still require a constitutional amendment approved by two-thirds of both chambers and then voters.

Texas state control is now divided. While Democrats flipped the state House, Republicans kept the Senate. Democrat Gina Hinojosa won the governorship.

Las Vegas Sands spokesman Ron Reese told CNBC it's too early to speculate about what might happen with Texas gaming, but that the company will monitor developments closely.

Truist gaming analyst Barry Jonas wrote in a Wednesday note that he sees mixed implications from the midterm results.

"The Ohio governor-elect Amy Acton's support for regulated online casinos could improve the chances of iGaming legalization," Jonas said. On the other hand, Nebraska voters rejected ballot measures to legalize online betting.

Georgia, where Democrat Keisha Lance Bottoms won the governorship, could also provide an opportunity. She campaigned on letting voters decide whether to legalize casino gambling, with revenue expected to be earmarked to help fund education. Sports betting and casinos remain illegal in the state, and earlier sports-betting legislation failed in the House.

Penn could also benefit if lawmakers seek tighter regulations for so-called skill-based gaming machines, which compete with casinos and licensed video gaming terminals, according to Snowden. He said candidates who support skill-based gaming machines lost seats or influence in several states.

Penn operates four casinos in Pennsylvania as well as video gaming terminals in that state and other jurisdictions, and has three casinos in Missouri.

Snowden linked recent strong year-over-year gaming revenue growth to the shutdown of skill games in Pennsylvania in mid-October. Although he cautioned that it was too early to know whether Pennsylvania’s growth would last, he noted Missouri also saw strong revenue growth as its state attorney general pursued the machines there.

In Indiana, voters in three counties rejected a proposed casino license, which Penn sees as a benefit as it limits new competition for existing casinos in Indiana and neighboring Illinois and Ohio. Some gaming insiders speculate that a wave of left-leaning progressives in power could work to limit gambling businesses.

"There's no shortage of noise about where this industry is headed, and the election certainly turns up the volume," said Joe Maloney, president of Sports Betting Alliance. "What hasn't changed with the election is what works, and that's state-regulated sports betting and iGaming, built on accountability, consumer protection, and tax revenue for states. That's our lane, and we'll make that case to every new lawmaker willing to listen."

Democratic victories are likely to increase the pressure on prediction markets, even as industry supporters call out their predictive value in forecasting the election results.

"Every eligible customer should have access to these markets, which are federally regulated through our CFTC-registered Futures Commission Merchant," a Robinhood spokesperson said.

Democrats’ control of Congress gives them subpoena power and committee resources to investigate the president’s family and business dealings. That could put Donald Trump Jr. back in the spotlight as he is a paid strategic adviser to Kalshi and sits on Polymarket’s advisory board.

Congress could also consider legislation limiting the markets’ scope. The bipartisan Prediction Markets Are Gambling Act would bar Commodity Futures Trading Commission-regulated exchanges from offering contracts based on sports or casino-style games. But the bill had only been referred to committee as of the election. A veto-proof law would require two-thirds support in both chambers; no such vote is currently scheduled.

The American Gaming Association has been fighting prediction markets as unlicensed gambling.

"The prediction markets are facing a tsunami of criminal charges, major civil investigations and significant defeats in court," Bill Miller, the trade group's CEO, told CNBC. "In the aftermath of the election, we expect the bipartisan scrutiny of these platforms to further intensify."

"Our platform is an institutional-grade exchange with strong market-integrity and customer-protection programs," Polymarket told CNBC. A spokesman said the company welcomes the opportunity to work with lawmakers on both sides of the aisle.

Repeated requests to Kalshi for comment went unanswered.

Jeff LeRiche, a partner at law firm Husch Blackwell, said he expects more scrutiny.

"Entities with robust compliance and surveillance infrastructure will be well positioned to navigate the increased attention," said LeRiche, who spent more than 20 years in the CFTC's enforcement division.

The state-level legal fight is already underway. Utah has filed criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit rejected Kalshi’s request for a rehearing in its Nevada case, leaving the company exposed to state enforcement there.

New Democratic attorneys general in Ohio, Michigan and Nevada will also decide how to handle inherited disputes.

Ohio’s outgoing AG Andy Wilson helped lead a multistate effort to defend state gambling laws, Michigan’s Dana Nessel sued Kalshi and won a preliminary injunction, and Nevada’s Aaron Ford secured injunctions against prediction-market operators.

New AGs could also decide to follow Utah's lead in filing criminal charges, along with Arizona where criminal charges are pending.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2967. Nico Rosberg compares Ferrari’s clashes to his own Mercedes F1 rivalry: “The instructions are not clear enough”

Nico Rosberg knows a thing or two about racing Lewis Hamilton wheel to wheel in sister Formula 1 cars.

The pair were team-mates for four years at Mercedes with an intense rivalry, and now Hamilton competes for Ferrari alongside Charles Leclerc – which has become tense at times, amid disagreements over team orders and on-track clashes.

Hamilton ended up in the gravel at the start in Monza, while on lap one in Mexico they both went off the track while battling for the lead, paving the way for Max Verstappen to snatch victory away from the Scuderia.

Ferrari set for crunch meeting as Lewis Hamilton and Charles Leclerc tension reaches Brazil

While team principal Fred Vasseur sternly expressed his frustration, Rosberg doesn't reckon the Scuderia has been handling the issue particularly well.

Speaking about the 2016 Monaco Grand Prix, when he let title rival Hamilton through to chase Daniel Ricciardo for victory, Rosberg explained Mercedes had instilled the right conditions for healthier cooperation between team-mates.

"It was very easy, because Toto [Wolff] and the team did a very good job clarifying the team racing guidelines," the 2016 world champion told the Beyond The Grid podcast. "It's very clear: if the team is looking to lose the race win, then the moment comes when you need to drive as a team and not anymore as championship rivals. It was very clear.

"In Monaco, I was not going well because my visor was so fogged up I couldn't see where I was going. I was very slow, Ricciardo was disappearing in the distance, so it was very clear there was a threat that we're losing the win here. So it's very clear then you need to drive as a team and Lewis needs to have a shot at it, so wave him past.

"That was super painful, but it's not so bad when the instructions are clear beforehand, and that's so important, that the instructions are clear beforehand, which is something that Ferrari for example is not doing well enough at the moment in F1, because the instructions are not clear enough, and then you get this arguing between the drivers. It just needs to be clear.

"Also, we didn't do it perfectly at the time either. We did in this case, but what we didn't do perfectly was wheel-to-wheel battles. You can never be enough into details as to wheel-to-wheel battles to make it very clear who's at fault when, and we didn't go into details enough.

"We tried, we were quite well advanced in printing our own rulebook for wheel-to-wheel battles, but it was not detailed enough, and there was always ambiguity, every single time – also because Lewis is so damn smart in wheel-to-wheel racing, so it was never 100% his fault, and sometimes it would be 100% my fault."

Discounting instances where they 'merely' pushed each other off the track, Hamilton and Rosberg actually collided on three occasions during their time at the team – the 2014 Belgian GP and the 2016 Spanish and Austrian GP. Victory eluded them twice then.

Mercedes did eventually implement a simple rule to deter them from unnecessarily clashing: they would have to share the cost of any collision between the Silver Arrows – which Rosberg says cost him $360,000 on one occasion.

The German explained Mercedes F1 non-executive chairman Niki Lauda had played a big role in maintaining a level of harmony between him and Hamilton.

Lewis Hamilton, Mercedes AMG F1 W07 Hybrid and Nico Rosberg, Mercedes AMG F1 W07 Hybrid collide on the opening lap

"Paddy [Lowe, executive director] is a risk-averse person," Rosberg detailed. "He tries to avoid confrontation as a human, so not ideal to have him managing such a post-incident discussion, because he would take, very often, the easiest path – not like go into it properly, confronting us, he would choose the path of least resistance, which is not ideal.

"It was better when Toto and Niki got involved. Niki got involved quite a few times; he was a great mediator, because we all appreciated him.

"He was very good at getting each of us to own a little bit of it at least, and that brought us closer. When it wasn't just 100% the other guy's fault, Niki would get both of us to [say], 'Yeah OK, that part I agree, maybe that was a bit too late that I closed the door'. And then Lewis would say, 'Yeah, I agree, I went too crazy aggressive on the grass', or whatever.

"That kind of started to move us together and to a way that we could then sit us together and say, 'OK, let's try and move on from this'."

Lewis Hamilton criticises Charles Leclerc: 'It was the same with Sebastian Vettel, the same with Carlos Sainz'

#2966. What we learned as Steph Curry catches fire in second half as Warriors burn Heat

What we learned as Steph Curry catches fire in second half as Warriors burn Heat originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – The team that had two days of rest looked much more like a team that played into overtime the night before in the early portions of the Warriors' game against the Miami Heat on Thursday night at Chase Center.

Slowly but surely, the Warriors found a second wind that wasn't available to the Heat, beating them 120-100. Their first lead of the game came from a Steph Curry layup at the 1:38 mark of the second quarter.

They outscored the Heat by 15 points in the third quarter and maintained the lead in the fourth quarter for a sixth straight win.

Curry continued his reign, scoring a game-high 29 points, with 22 coming after halftime. His running mate, Draymond Green, did a fantastic job defensively and engineering the Warriors' offense.

Former Warriors Klay Thompson (11 points) and Andrew Wiggins (two) combined for 13 points.

Here are three takeaways from the Warriors' win to keep rolling.

They shot on opposite sides of the court at the same time during their pregame routines and caught up for a moment together at half-court. The moment was just the start to what would be a long night for former longtime teammates Curry and Thompson. As the Heat won the opening tip, Curry went right to Thompson for his defensive matchup.

That was the area Thompson made an immediate impact, too. He played so well on Brandin Podziemski that Steve Kerr had to make a change faster than usual. Thompson even swatted Curry at the rim in the first quarter after getting beat by him on a back cut.

Neither got it going offensively in the first half. Curry scored just seven points on a lowly 2-of-10 shooting, and a three late in the second quarter were Thompson's only points. Curry splashed a three within the first minute of the third quarter, but also fouled Thompson on a three the next time down.

But the third quarter has been all Curry's this season, and he continued his tear against his former Splash Brother. Curry scored 14 points and made three 3-pointers in the quarter to help the Warriors secure a 15-point lead going into the fourth.

Curry now is shooting 61.9 percent from three in the third quarter (13 of 21). He added another eight points in the fourth quarter to fall one point shy of his fourth straight 30-point game.

When Green came apart last game and was ejected against the Suns, the Warriors came together to complete a comeback win. There were many more storylines outside of Green going into the game, but he was sure to get up for a game matching up against Giannis Antetokounmpo and Co.

Green dared Antetokounmpo to shoot from deep and was active around the rim, whether it was with the former Milwaukee Bucks superstar, his former Warriors teammate Andrew Wiggins or anybody else. His level of focus was night-and-day different from Monday night.

This was a game where you had to watch closely to understand Green's impact. Look beyond the box score with him. But some numbers certainly stick out, too.

Antetokounmpo scored 20 points but was a minus-22. Green's plus-13 was second among Warriors starters, behind just Curry, and his 11 assists were five more than his next closest teammate. The Warriors had a 96.2 defensive rating, and Green is the main reason why.

Sure, Bam Adebayo put him on roller skates in the second quarter, but Al Horford got him back in the third quarter and ultimately had the final laugh in another Warriors win.

And, that wasn't even Horford's best highlight from the game. Or the quarter, for that matter.

Horford, 40, flew in for a two-handed reverse tip-in layup that made him look closer to his jersey number (20) than his age.

The 20-year vet has scored more points this season than he did Thursday night. He's grabbed more rebounds and made more threes. But in his strategic minutes, he hasn't meant as much to the Warriors as he did against the Heat, finishing as a game-high plus-21 in a game when he was a perfect 3 of 3 from the field and 2 of 2 from deep.

#2965. Dynasties or Parity? How MLB, the NFL, and the NBA try to find competitive balance

MLB owners and players are preparing for a likely lockout, and potentially missed games in 2027, over the contentious issue of adopting a salary cap. Owners are fighting to keep payrolls in check, and players want to get paid what they think they deserve.

But don't be confused — this isn't a referendum on curbing sports dynasties.

"We don't think dynasties are bad," said Morgan Sword, MLB's executive vice president of baseball operations, in an interview with CNBC Sport. "Dynasties that are achieved on well-run organizations can be wonderful for a league and engage people outside of that city."

Earlier this week, MLB proposed a cap of $258.2 million and a floor of $180.2 million to start in 2028. Limiting the spending ranges of teams should make the league inherently more fair, according to MLB executives. Currently, teams can spend an unlimited amount on players without penalty. The New York Mets and L.A. Dodgers spent more than $335 million on their 2026 rosters while the Miami Marlins and Cleveland Guardians spent less than $90 million, according to USA Today data.

Still, reasonable people can argue both sides of whether there's actually a competitive balance problem in baseball. MLBPA head Bruce Meyer has repeatedly pointed to metrics, such as the varied array of recent World Series winners — 10 different teams in the last 13 years — and the success of mid- or small-market, low-spending teams such as the Guardians, Tampa Bay Rays and Brewers, that prove the point that a cap is simply not needed.

Whether a league should value dynasties over parity, or vice versa, to improve fan engagement is a topic for debate. NBA Commissioner Adam Silver has hedged on this issue. In a CNBC Sport interview in 2024, he suggested parity was a superior outcome to dynasties.

"The data is absolutely crystal clear that the more competition you have, the more it drives interest in the league," Silver said in 2024. "If you have a 30-team league and you only have four teams competing for championships, of course the fans of those teams love that, and the fans of those teams that have been historic dynasties have disproportionate amounts of fans. But, we think competition is great. That's ultimately what we're providing to our fans."

Still, in an interview with "The Dan Patrick Show" in June, Silver noted dynasties can be "great" as long as fans agree that league rules are fair for all competitors.

"I don't think fans are as enthusiastic about them if one team is spending drastically more than another team," Silver said.

The NBA changed its salary cap before the 2024-25 season to include a second apron that disincentivizes spending above a certain threshold with harsh penalties that restrict free agency and trades. Only one NBA team — the Denver Nuggets — spent over the second apron this season, set at $221.7 million for 2026-27.

Silver credits the change with a move toward emphasizing parity over dynasties — a topic he discussed in 2024 with CNBC Sport after the second apron's introduction.

The NBA has had eight different champions in the past eight seasons. Even the losing teams in the most recent NBA Finals have been varied. The last nine runners-up: the San Antonio Spurs, Indiana Pacers, Dallas Mavericks, Miami Heat, Boston Celtics, Phoenix Suns, Heat, Golden State Warriors, and the Cleveland Cavaliers.

Both MLB and NBA league executives have said they view the NFL's hard cap setup as the gold standard in sports competitive balance.

"The goal over time has been, and I've said it, to be more NFL-like, where you believe your team has a shot … regardless of the market size, " Silver said in June. "Fans want a sense that [the league construction] is not top heavy, as it was historically in the NBA. Remember, three teams won 60% of the championships over the first 65 years of this league."

There's some irony in both leagues pointing to the NFL as the prototype for competitive balance. The New England Patriots had a 20-year dynasty from 2001 to 2021 with quarterback Tom Brady and head coach Bill Belichick. Barring significant injuries, teams like the Kansas City Chiefs, Buffalo Bills and San Francisco 49ers have recently been Super Bowl contenders every season while bottom dwellers such as the Arizona Cardinals and New York Jets seem to struggle year after year.

There's an argument the hard cap is actually a detriment to competitive balance.

A cap "takes competition out of the hands of clubs and prevents teams from getting better," NFLPA chief Bruce Meyer said to The Athletic last month. "It certainly doesn't guarantee better balance, and we know that from the dynasties in the other sports."

But Sword argued the existence of dynasties in the NFL is actually a selling point, not a flaw.

"It's frustrating for fans where the Kansas City Royals don't have the same opportunity to build a dynasty as the Kansas City Chiefs because of the economic system of the leagues," Sword said.

MLB's point of view on dynasties is they can be "wonderful" for a league, he said.

"I believe fans love excellence," said Sword. "If fans think the game is being played on a fair set of rules, fans love seeing groups of people excel under that set of rules. I think fans enjoy the whole arch of a dynasty — the construction of one, the reign of one, and the fall of one."

It's that last point that's key to MLB's argument for a cap — the fall of one. MLB fears its uncapped rules allow for well-run teams to achieve sustained successes without ever having to worry about the fall of a dynasty.

Much of the argument in the coming months for or against a cap will have to do with whether or not the players feel they are advantaged or disadvantaged by a cap. But the issue of whether caps and floors lead to a better fan experience is perhaps even more important for a league's health.

#2964. Mississippi Senate: Colom, Hyde-Smith gird for ballot legal fight in razor-thin race

An ultra-tight Mississippi Senate race between Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith and Democratic challenger Scott Colom could end with one of the biggest shocks in a midterm election cycle already chock full of stunning defeats of GOP candidates.

With Colom in the lead by just 1,813 votes on Thursday, Hyde-Smith's campaign set the stage for a potential legal battle over ballots by demanding in a letter to county commissioners and circuit clerks in all 82 Mississippi counties that they immediately separate all provisional ballots cast in the election from regular and absentee ballots.

"These measures are necessary to ensure that each affidavit ballot's eligibility for counting andultimate disposition can be verified," the letter said.

Lawyers for Colom's campaign responded with a letter of their own to the counties, calling the Republican's demands "unlawful and extraordinary measures."

"Those demands have no basis in Mississippi law, and if you depart from ordinary election procedures as you have been asked to do, you will violate Mississippi voters' rights and Mississippi election law," they said.

Provisional ballots, which are also known as affidavit ballots, are cast by a voter whose name does not appear on the poll book, who does not present an acceptable photo ID, or in several other situations.

The ballot is then placed in a sealed envelope and later examined by election officials to see if it can be cast, or the voter can return with a photo ID or evidence within five business days to show they are entitled to cast the ballot.

When the letters were sent, Colom had 49.04% of the counted ballots, compared to 48.85% for Hyde-Smith, with an estimated more than 95% of the votes in, according to the Associated Press.

Colom's lead ticked up slightly late Thursday afternoon to 1,813 votes out of a total of 951,744 counted as of 6:17 p.m. ET, the AP said. Ty Pinkins, an independent candidate, had 20,007 votes, or 2.1% of the total count.

A final result may not be available until next week because more ballots can still arrive and be counted.

Mississippi's election procedures do not have an automatic recount provision.

"Absolutely, I didn't think it would be this close," said Brian Anderson, a political science professor at the Mississippi University for Women in Columbus.

Anderson said that before the election, he thought Colom, a 43-year-old district attorney, might get into position to lose by just 3 or 4 percentage points, which would have been a very solid showing in a state that hasn't elected a Democrat to the Senate since 1982.

"I'm much more surprised" now that Colom has a chance to win, Anderson said.

If Colom defeats Hyde-Smith, 67, he would be the first Black senator from Mississippi since the post-Civil War Reconstruction Era.

"The Democrats have struggled here to create a pipeline of candidates," Anderson said. A Colom win "would galvanize the Democratic Party in a way it has not been," he said.

Neither candidate's campaign immediately responded to requests for comment from CNBC.

Hyde-Smith's X account, in a post Thursday afternoon, said, "Cindy Hyde-Smith is going to represent Mississippi in the United States Senate for the next six years."

"We believe thousands of votes remain to be counted from dozens of Republican counties, and we are confident those votes will reinforce her path to victory," the post said. "Election officials are continuing the normal process of counting outstanding ballots, including absentee ballots. They must have the time to complete that work and ensure every lawful ballot is accurately counted and every Mississippian's voice is heard."

The post added, "We expect Democratic lawyers to pull out every stop, and we are prepared. Our focus is on completing the count and returning Cindy Hyde-Smith to the Senate to keep fighting for Mississippi values."

"Mississippi is the firewall, and this race is our line in the sand," the message said.

In a pair of ironic twists, if Colom wins, his victory could be attributed in some ways to actions taken by two Republicans: President Donald Trump and Hyde-Smith herself.

Supreme Court Justice Amy Coney Barrett, whom Trump appointed, wrote the court's 5-4 majority ruling in June that upheld a Mississippi law allowing mail-in absentee ballots postmarked by Election Day to be counted if they are received within five business days.

Those ballots could provide Colom a margin of victory. There are also an estimated 10,000 to 15,000 provisional ballots that require verification of eligibility to vote.

A lawyer for the Republican National Committee, whose lawsuit argued federal law barred counting votes received after Election Day, noted in a brief to the Supreme Court, "Democratic voters tend to mail their ballots later on average than Republican voters, which results in late-arriving ballots favoring Democratic candidates."

Trump at the time of the Supreme Court ruling called it "a tremendous loss" for voters' rights.

Hyde-Smith was appointed to her Senate seat in 2018 after the resignation of Thad Cochran and later that year won a special election for the seat. She was elected to a full term in 2020.

In 2023, she effectively blocked Colom's nomination by then-President Joe Biden to a post as a federal judge for the Northern District of Mississippi. Colom's nomination had the support of Mississippi's other Republican senator, Roger Wicker, and two former GOP governors.

Hyde-Smith said she opposed Colom's nomination for reasons that included his purported support for transgender students participating in girls' sports and because his campaign for district attorney had allegedly received money from billionaire George Soros.

Colom, in an April 10, 2023, letter to Hyde-Smith, said he was unaware before his hometown newspaper reported it that Soros had donated to a political action committee that had supported his campaign. He also wrote that he was not sure why Hyde-Smith had suggested he "opposed legislation to protect female athletes."

Colom concluded the letter by promising to be guided by the law, not his personal views on public policy, if he were confirmed as a judge.

"I hope you will reconsider your views about my nomination," he wrote.

Hyde-Smith did not reconsider her views and maintained her block on the nomination.

Colom announced his candidacy for the Democratic nomination for Senate in September 2025 and recently told CNN, "Me not having a federal judgeship is not a motivating factor for me at all" in running.

Anderson said Hyde-Smith ran "a low-visibility campaign," at least compared to Colom's, and that she focused on voters who traditionally would be expected to support her.

Hyde-Smith refused to debate Colom several times.

Colom "knows how to speak to people's interests, and Hyde-Smith was all about values, which is great for a lot of people, but it's all about the interests right now," Anderson said.

Those interests include concerns about affordability, the Iran war and corruption.

Colom is "bringing up all those," Anderson said.

Anderson noted that Colom's campaign had pounced on a remark Hyde-Smith made during a Feb. 22 interview with a local television outlet.

In the interview, Hyde-Smith answered a question about rising beef prices by saying, "You know, there so many proteins to choose from."

Colom's campaign featured a video clip of that remark in an ad featuring people reacting incredulously to Hyde-Smith's suggestion.

"Pick another protein?" one woman said in the ad. "She done lost her d--- mind. I'd rather pick another senator."

#2963. WTA apologizes after Jessica Pegula caught on hot mic calling Mirra Andreeva a ‘diva’ on livestream

The WTA apologized Thursday for failing to clearly tell players and coaches that livestream microphones were recording practice sessions at Indian Wells.

The apology came after a hot mic caught Jessica Pegula talking about Mirra Andreeva and calling her a "diva" during a conversation with one of her coaches, Mark Knowles, which they both believed was private.

However, according to The Athletic, the session was being livestreamed on the WTA Tour's YouTube channel.

During the conversation, Pegula said she had "lost a little respect" for 1994 Wimbledon champion Conchita Martínez, Andreeva's coach, because she pokes (Andreeva) and eggs on her behavior.

"I think she's a nice girl; she's just a diva. She needs to be humbled," Pegula said of the 19-year-old French Open champion.

Pegula also compared Andreeva to Coco Gauff, saying Andreeva could surpass Gauff by adopting her "mentality."

The comments came following a practice session in which Pegula played against Andreeva. The Russian took four straight games from Pegula during the session, per The Athletic, but she also grew annoyed and started hitting balls toward her coach, Martínez. That behavior is apparently what irked Pegula.

In a statement, the WTA apologized for "failing to meet our responsibility to ensure players and coaches were properly informed."

A representative for Pegula told The Athletic that the tennis star "had absolutely no idea that her conversation with Mark was being livestreamed and certainly never expected a private discussion during practice to be made public."

The WTA has since removed all practice streams from this year's event from the platform. However, future practice sessions remain scheduled to be livestreamed on the WTA YouTube page. A Thursday practice session between Taylor Townsend and Katerina Siniakova was livestreamed without audio.

This is the latest in a series of incidents involving professional tennis players and audio or video access to their actions that they would rather keep private.

#2962. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2961. AI debt concerns grow as ING reportedly seeks buyers for $500 million of data center loans

ING is reportedly seeking buyers for a portfolio of 12 data center loans worth around $500 million, joining a broader push by banks to distribute credit risk tied to the artificial intelligence infrastructure build-out.

The auction is said to include a $25 million piece linked to the Stargate project in New Mexico, which involves OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle, as well as certain other loan assets involving Vantage Data Centers and CyrusOne, Bloomberg reported Friday, citing people familiar with the matter.

ING will hold a bids-wanted-in-competition, or BWIC, auction process, with bids sized at $10 million or more due by Nov. 19, according to Bloomberg. A BWIC is a competitive process for investors to bid on pieces of a loan portfolio.

The sale highlights a growing market for banks to move data center loans to private credit firms, asset managers and insurers as financing for the AI boom expands.

Investment banks typically use a lend-to-distribute model for these types of loans, said Hank Calenti, global markets strategist at SMBC.

"The buyers can range from private credit investors through asset managers to insurers. They would buy these assets for their expected higher returns," he said.

The ING sale comes as fears grow over soaring debt tied to the AI build-out — and potentially disappointing returns among investors.

Those concerns have accelerated following the Democrats' sweeping win in the U.S. midterms, which could see moratoriums or tighter restrictions on data center construction in certain states.

The perceived risk of a construction slowdown sent AI-related stocks into free fall on Thursday, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index closing the session down 5.5% — its worst day since April 2025 — and into correction territory.

"Whether banks sell more based on AI data centers moratorium fears should be driven by the cost (discount received) versus the benefit of exiting to free up cash for further lending, where the cost includes explaining the exposure to your board," Calenti told CNBC via email.

"This could be business as usual, or financiers looking to exit before the door shuts."

Pruksa Iamthongthong, head of APAC equities at Aberdeen Investments, said the Democrats' gains have increased concerns around greater scrutiny of data center development, at a time when investors were already questioning the financing and returns behind the scale of AI capital spending.

"We think near-term volatility could continue as the market assesses potential delays to U.S. data center construction and constraints around compute capacity."

Earlier this year, Wall Street lenders including JPMorgan Chase and Morgan Stanley were said to be exploring a variety of approaches to reduce their lending exposures to data center construction.

According to a Financial Times report on May 3, these include offloading portions of debt via private sales to non-bank lenders, as well as through so-called significant risk transfers, which transfer part of a bank's credit risk to investors to free up balance sheet capacity.

#2960. Montana votes to ban corporate political money. Next stop? Probably the Supreme Court

Imagine a corporation wants to spend $10 million to defeat a political candidate. Under the Supreme Court's landmark 2010 Citizens United ruling, it can spend as much as it wants provided it doesn't coordinate with the candidate's campaign.

On Tuesday, Montana voted to change that.

In a state President Donald Trump won by nearly 20 points in 2024, roughly 65% of Montanans voted to approve Initiative 194 – the "Montana Plan" – embracing a novel legal strategy to curb corporate political spending. If states cannot restrict political speech under Citizens United, can they instead strip corporations of the power to spend money on elections?

The vote follows a record $11.87 billion in political advertising nationwide this election cycle that was split about evenly between the two parties, according to media tracking firm AdImpact.

The voter-approved measure is set to become state law on Jan. 1 in a state where voters can make law via ballot initiatives. Montana's Republican-controlled legislature could amend or repeal it, though if that happens, proponents have pledged to take the fight back to voters in 2028.

Now, Montana is poised for a constitutional showdown that could reach the nation's highest court. If the strategy survives, it could provide a replicable blueprint for restricting corporate political spending across the country. Similar measures are being explored in more than 30 states.

"If a single state can successfully deny corporate power over elections, it proves that Citizens United wasn't the final word on democracy, and we will continue battling," said Tom Moore, a former Federal Election Commission official and current senior fellow at the Center for American Progress who helped develop the Montana Plan.

The Montana Plan hinges on a legal distinction: Corporations may have constitutional rights, but their powers come from the states.

Under Initiative 194, Montana would strip corporations, trade associations, labor unions and nonprofits, including those headquartered elsewhere, of the legal power to spend money influencing elections in the state.

In practice, that should mean no corporate-funded attack ads, no trade associations bankrolling referendum campaigns and no politically active nonprofits spending their own funds to sway Montana voters.

Violators risk losing state-granted corporate privileges like limited liability, which generally protects shareholders from personal responsibility for corporate debts, until all improperly spent funds are surrendered and legal conditions are met.

"The argument of the people supporting the initiative is, in theory, trying to be pretty simple. Keep your campaign cash out of Montana, or you run the risk of putting your shareholders' personal balance sheets on the line," said Kal Munis, a politics professor at Montana State University who is an expert in the state's politics.

The financial stakes extend well beyond Montana. Known corporate contributions to outside groups in federal elections surged from $13 million in 2010 to more than $600 million by September 2026, according to OpenSecrets, a nonpartisan nonprofit that tracks such spending. That excludes money funneled through nonprofits that conceal their donors.

But there's an irony to the Montana Plan. It had high-dollar backers from corporate America.

State campaign finance data shows Samantha Walton, wife of Walmart heir Lukas Walton, gave $722,300 to groups backing the plan, while LinkedIn co-founder and Democratic megadonor Reid Hoffman contributed $150,000. The campaign's organizing nonprofit, Transparent Election Initiative, is itself a type of dark-money group that generally isn't required to disclose its donors.

And the new law wouldn't exactly take billionaires out of politics. It exempts individuals, political committees, government entities and bona fide news, commentary and editorial content.

Consider Tesla and SpaceX CEO Elon Musk, the 2026 election's largest known individual donor, with at least $474 million in political giving, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records. Musk could still spend millions of his personal fortune influencing Montana elections. His companies, however, would face the new restrictions.

Moore argued that because corporations derive their legal existence from states, those states should have the power to restrict their political spending.

Opponents call the Montana Plan an unconstitutional end run around Citizens United. If states cannot directly prohibit protected political speech, they argue, they cannot accomplish the same thing by threatening companies' legal privileges.

And critics warn the restrictions would reach beyond corporate giants, silencing local trade groups, labor unions and community organizations on school bonds, tax measures and other local ballot issues.

"Why allow a media corporation to speak out, while denying a non-profit association of citizens the right to organize around clean water or lower taxes?" said Brad Smith, who leads the Institute for Free Speech and is a former FEC Chairman. "Billionaires will find loopholes. All this really does is silence Montana's small- and medium-sized businesses."

The first legal test is already underway in Hawaii, where lawmakers enacted a similar law in May. Montana is the first state to embrace the strategy through a statewide vote.

The Hawaii law faces formidable legal opposition. The U.S. Chamber of Commerce has sued to block it, while Smith's Institute for Free Speech is representing the Grassroot Institute of Hawaii in a separate challenge. The national ACLU has also weighed in, arguing in a court brief that the law violates the First Amendment.

Legal and campaign finance experts told CNBC the dispute could eventually reach the Supreme Court, whose 6-3 conservative majority has repeatedly loosened campaign finance restrictions. Just in June, the justices struck down limits on political parties' spending in coordination with candidates.

"Shifting the prohibition into the corporate code is a distinction without a difference," said Michael Kang, a Northwestern Pritzker School of Law professor who studies campaign finance. "The courts will see right through it because the clear intent remains to bypass Citizens United. It seems like a legal sleight of hand."

Montana has lost this fight before. In 1912, voters banned corporate campaign spending after copper barons bribed lawmakers and dominated state politics. The law stood for a century before the Supreme Court struck it down in 2012 under Citizens United. This time, the threat could also come from within: Montana's Republican-controlled legislature could weaken or repeal the new law.

"The way we look at it, this is states' rights vs Citizens United," Moore said. "That's the pitch once and if it makes it to the Supreme Court they're going to need to decide between one of their two favorite legal theories and things to fall back on."

Who: Phoenix Suns (1-5) vs. Orlando Magic (2-5)

Where: Mortgage Matchup Center — Phoenix, Arizona

Watch: Arizona's Family 3TV, Arizona's Family Sports

The Suns are back at it tonight after a loss to the Trail Blazers last night. The homestand continues.

Four straight losses. 1-5. And the Suns just watched Portland drop 43 on them in a single quarter after winning the first one 34-29.

I'll say it out loud: game 7 of an 82-game season is a must-win. That sounds insane from the outside. It isn't. Houston and San Antonio come to town right after this, and if Phoenix can't beat a 2-5 Orlando team missing Paolo Banchero, I have no idea where the next win comes from.

Through six games, nothing on film says this team is better than its record. The ball movement in the first quarter in the last two games showed what the ceiling looks like. NBA games are 48 minutes long; it needs to be there for more than 12 or 15 minutes. If Jordan Ott's group can't defend a bottom-10 offense at home, the conversation shifts from "early-season growing pains" to "roster construction problem," and the lineup experimentation has to start now.

#2958. Would the CFP committee save Texas after loss to Missouri? Wrighster weighs in

George Wrighster posted a clip Thursday with a prediction about how the College Football Playoff selection committee would treat the Texas Longhorns if they lose at the Missouri Tigers on Saturday. The former NFL tight end and Unafraid Show host did not give the committee much benefit of the doubt.

"If Missouri beats Texas, the CFP committee will even screw somebody else over to get Texas in the playoff," Wrighster said.

Texas and Missouri both arrive at Faurot Field at 6-2 and 3-2 in SEC play, and the committee's first rankings Tuesday put them 11 spots apart. Texas debuted at No. 7. Missouri landed at No. 18.

The only two teams to beat Missouri are the Ole Miss Rebels, 31-3 in Oxford, and the Mississippi State Bulldogs, 31-24 in Starkville. Texas beat both of them in its last two games, 34-27 over Ole Miss and 31-3 over Mississippi State.

The Longhorns also own a 24-23 win over the Ohio State Buckeyes and a 20-17 win at the Tennessee Volunteers, while Missouri's best result is a 27-6 home win over the Texas A&M Aggies. Texas' two losses, to the Oklahoma Sooners and the Florida Gators, both came by three points.

Even with the logos removed, those two resumes would give the committee reasons to put Texas ahead. Wrighster's claim is about what happens after a loss, when Missouri would hold the head-to-head result.

Last December, the committee left a 9-3 Texas team out of the field. The Longhorns finished with three wins over teams in the committee's top 15 and still landed at No. 13 in the final rankings, behind the Notre Dame Fighting Irish and BYU Cougars.

The same committee did take a three-loss SEC team that day. The Alabama Crimson Tide went in at No. 9 with a 10-3 record, but its third loss came in the SEC Championship Game, a game Texas never reached.

A loss in Columbia would drop Texas to 6-3 with a trip to the LSU Tigers, a home game against the Arkansas Razorbacks and a road game at Texas A&M left. Running that table gets the Longhorns to 9-3, the exact record that left them out a year ago. The difference this time would be the win over Ohio State, and that is the piece of the resume Wrighster expects the committee to lean on..

The Tigers' remaining schedule after Saturday runs through a trip to the Georgia Bulldogs and home games against the Kentucky Wildcats and Oklahoma, and a three-loss Missouri team would have far less margin than Texas has shown it gets from this committee.

Kickoff in Columbia is at noon ET. If Missouri wins, the rest of Wrighster's theory gets tested over the final three weeks of the regular season.

#2957. Market sell-off hits hard: Advisors say 'stay the course with an eye on buying'

Top financial advisors aren't urging clients to change their portfolios after Thursday's sharp market sell-off, even as retail investors were heavy sellers of individual stocks.

In a Thursday flash poll of advisors from CNBC's Financial Advisor 100 and Financial Advisor Council, more than three-quarters of respondents said investors' first steps should be to check their allocation against their goals or stay the course. The online poll, conducted from 2:30 p.m. to 6 p.m. ET on Thursday, was sent to 121 firms and 38 responded.

Retail investors sold individual stocks Thursday as technology stocks tumbled, and the Dow gave back its post-midterm election gains. The S&P 500 suffered its worst one-day drop since April 2025 on Thursday. Analysts linked the sell-off to fears of a data center construction slowdown following major Democratic election wins.

The Nasdaq Composite tumbled 5.5%, closing more than 10% below its most recent high and putting it in what Wall Street regards as a correction. The broad-market S&P 500 lost 4.4%, while the Dow fell more than 1,800 points, or 3.4%.

Yet, financial advisors who answered the CNBC poll overwhelmingly responded that they are not recommending changes to their clients' portfolios. The broader message from interviews with several advisors: review whether your portfolio still matches your goals, but don't let a sudden market move make the decision for you.

For investors who continue contributing to a 401(k), a market decline can mean buying investments at lower prices over time, said Andy Pratt, director of investment strategy at Burney Wealth Management in Reston, Virginia.

"If you have a 401(k), money is being allocated there biweekly, monthly, you know, whatever your pay period is, and so you're able to dollar-cost average in," Pratt said. "So when the market does sell off, you're entering at a much better spot."

Pratt said a well-designed portfolio may not need changes during a downturn. Certified financial planner John Mullen, president and CEO of Parsons Capital Management in Providence, Rhode Island, put it this way: "Stay the course with an eye on buying."

Yet, if investors are panicking, that could be a sign to reassess whether their investments still fit their goals and risk tolerance, financial advisors say.

Carolyn McClanahan, a CFP, physician and founder of Life Planning Partners in Jacksonville, Florida, says she tells clients to follow this strategy, which "works beautifully."

McClanahan said investors should stick to an investment policy that reflects their needs and "tune out the rest of the noise."

Tra Sullivan, a CFP, wealth manager, and chief investment officer at Dallas-based Beaird Harris Wealth Management, said historically the U.S. stock market has often risen in the 12 months after midterm elections, but he views that pattern as context rather than a promise.

He added that when markets fall, "there's always a recovery, and a lot of times, in most cases, more quickly than we anticipate", although past rebounds don't guarantee future recoveries.

Nasdaq futures rebounded in pre-market trading on Friday with a bounce in chip stocks following a sell-off that sent the Nasdaq into correction territory the day before.

Still, if market weakness continues, Sullivan said he brings the discussion back to a client's financial plan.

For long-term investors, Dan Moisand, a CFP and financial advisor at Orlando, Florida-based Moisand Fitzgerald Tamayo, said resilience can matter more than trying to time each market swing.

"Being resilient has proven to be more profitable than trying to be nimble," he said.

Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting to this article.

#2956. In Roob's Eagles Stats: A big pile of misery broken up by tiny encouraging blips

In Roob's Eagles Stats: A big pile of misery broken up by tiny encouraging blips originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Thank God for Makai Lemon and Eli Stowers. Because without those two rookies, I wouldn't have much of a "Positive Section" in this week's Roob's Eagles Stats.

Even with them there aren't a whole lot of encouraging numbers surrounding this football team.

So with that in mind, here's this week's Roob's Eagles Stats. When you lose six in a row, the stats aren't pretty.

1. With Makai Lemon catching six passes for 57 yards and Eli Stowers three for 53 yards, this was only the second time in the last 36 years the Eagles have had two rookies over 50 receiving yards in the same game. The last time it happened was in 2021, when DeVonta Smith had 122 yards and Kenny Gainwell 58 in a loss to the Chiefs at the Linc. Before that in 1990 Fred Barnett had 55 yards and Calvin Williams 72 in a win over the Patriots at the Vet. Two rookie replacement players did it in a 1987 strike replacement game against the Cowboys, including Haverford High's Mike Siano, but last time it happened in a real road game was in 1973 with Charlie Young (112) and Don Zimmerman (51) in a loss to the 49ers at Candlestick Park.

2. Lemon now has 31 catches, 3rd-most among NFL rookies and only two fewer than rookie leader K.C. Concepcion of the Browns, who has 33. Germie Bernard of the Steelers has 32. Lemon is on pace for 65 catches, which would be 3rd-most in Eagles history by a rookie, behind Keith Jackson (81 in 1988) and Jordan Matthews (67 in 2014). With seven catches against Dallas and six against Washington, Lemon tied the franchise record of consecutive games by a rookie with six or more receptions. Seven others have done it, most recently Jeremy Maclin in 2009 and DeVonta Smith in 2021.

3. With his fourth sack on Sunday night, Jalen Carter now has 11 ½ in his career and moved into a tie for 7th-most career sacks by an Eagles interior lineman. He's just 1 ½ behind Javon Hargrave for sixth. The top five are Andy Harmon and Fletcher Cox (20 ½), Jerome Brown (19), Darwin Walker (17) and Kenny Clarke (16 ½). With his fourth sack Nolan Smith surpassed his total of 3.0 from all of last year and is now just 2 ½ sacks shy of his career high of 6 ½ in 2024, not including four more from the postseason.

OK, thus begins the reality section. Brace yourself. This is bad. Really bad.

4. The Eagles have had fewer than 350 yards of offense in all eight games so far this year, with a high of 341 at Tennessee. This is the first time in more than half a century they've opened a season with eight games under 350 net yards. Last time it happened was the first 11 games of the 1971 season, when their yardage high was 315 yards in a 37-14 loss to the Bengals in the season opener at Riverfront Stadium. Going back to last year, the Eagles have gone 10 straight games with fewer than 350 yards, their longest streak since an 11-gamer over the last nine games of 1977 and the first games of 1978. This is not good.

5. The Eagles have been outscored by 65 points through eight games, the most points they've been outscored by in their first eight games since 1998, when they were outscored by 117 points. You have to go back to 1972 to find the last time before 1998 they were outscored by this many points eight games into a season. The only NFL team outscored by a larger margin this year is the 0-8 Chargers, who have been outscored by 76 points. The Eagles have been outscored by 11 points by the 3-4 Panthers and by 17 points by the 2-4-1 Commanders and 3-5 Cowboys.

6. This is the first time since 1970 the Eagles have lost three games in a season by 11 or more points to teams with losing records. It's only the third season in franchise history they've lost two games by 17 or more points to teams with losing records. It also happened in 1972 and 2015. The only other time they lost back-to-back games by at least 17 points to losing teams came in 2015, when they lost 45-17 to the Bucs, who finished 6-10, and 45-14 to a Lions team that finished 7-9. Chip Kelly was fired four weeks later.

7. The Eagles led 10-0 Sunday before losing 27-10. Last time the Eagles led a game by 10 points in the first quarter and lost by at least 17 points was in 2011, when they took a 10-0 lead on the Patriots at the Linc on a LeSean McCoy touchdown and Alex Henery field goal before getting outscored 38-10 the rest of the game and losing 38-20, Tom Brady over Vince Young. This was the first game in franchise history where the Eagles led by 10 points in the first quarter and didn't score again.

8. With a 25-14 loss to the Panthers, 38-21 to the Cowboys and 27-10 to the Commanders, this is only the third time in the last 50 years the Eagles have lost three straight games by 11 or more points, allowing at least 25 points in all three. It also happened in 2012 and 2016. Last time the Eagles were outscored by 71 points in a six-game span was 2012.

9. Rasul Douglas's interception near the end of the game was the 23rd of his 10-year career with the Eagles, Panthers, Packers, Cards, Bills, Dolphins and Commanders. Douglas is one of only three Eagles drafted in the last 30 years with at least 23 career interceptions. The others are Sheldon Brown (26) and Jordan Poyer (25). Douglas, the Eagles' 3rd-round pick in 2017, is the first cornerback drafted by the Eagles to record an interception against the Eagles since Mark McMillian of the Chiefs picked off Rodney Peete in a 24-21 Chiefs win at the Vet in 1998.

10. The Eagles netted just 268 yards Sunday night, their fifth game during the six-game losing streak with fewer than 270 yards. They're averaging 244 yards per game during these last six games, their worst six-game span in 27 years, since they averaged 241 during a six-game span in 1999, Andy Reid's first season as head coach. This is the first time they've had five games under 270 yards in a six-game span since early in the 2003 season. No other NFL team has had five games under 270 yards this year. Their overall average of 265 yards per game is their worst through eight games since 1999, when they were averaging 254 through their eighth game.

#2955. Nasdaq drops into correction territory — here’s what typically happens next

The Nasdaq Composite plunged into correction territory on Thursday. History shows more selling can follow in the near term, but a recovery eventually takes hold three months later.

The tech-heavy index slid more than 5% on Thursday, closing more than 11% below its record reached last month. On Wall Street, any decline greater than 10% is a correction. A 20% pullback qualifies as a bear market.

Across 10 previous correction episodes over the last decade, selloffs in 2025, 2022, 2020 and 2018 were followed by further rough selling over the next one month. But the Nasdaq was back to rally mode again after six months for all those occasions except one: 2022.

Overall, the Nasdaq gained an average of 9.41% from the correction bottom over the following three months and 17.03% over six months.

The January 2022 correction was the one that offered a harsher outcome. The Nasdaq lost another 18.3% over the next six months and was down 24.3% a year later.

The catalyst for this correction was Democrats sweeping the House and Senate in Tuesday's midterm elections. The party also made significant gains in Texas, where Gina Hinojosa won the race for governor after campaigning on an immediate moratorium for data center development.

Investors are now weighing whether tighter state-level restrictions could slow data center construction, putting pressure on companies tied to the artificial intelligence trade.

Frank Cappelleri, founder of CappThesis, pointed to past midterm election cycles for guidance and found that points to more volatility in the near term as well.

The chart analyst said the Nasdaq was higher 12 months after the election in all but one of 13 midterm cycles since 1974.

"The caveat is that the path can be a volatile one," he said.

Cappelleri pointed to 2018, when the Nasdaq rallied the day after Democrats won the House. A sharp December sell-off followed, but the index was ultimately about 15% higher a year after the election, he said.

Chip stocks tumbled Thursday, with the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX) falling 12% and for its worst day since the Covid-19 breakout of March 2020.

— CNBC's Fred Imbert contributed to the report

#2954. Bronny James assigned to G League's Coachella Valley Lakers after playing limited minutes early in third NBA season

Bronny James made the Los Angeles Lakers' 2026-27 opening night roster, securing a spot while his draft classmate, first-round pick Dalton Knecht, was waived.

But after James averaged 2.4 minutes in five appearances amid the Lakers' 7-2 start, the team announced Thursday that it assigned the 2024 second-round pick to the G League's Coachella Valley Lakers.

Fellow guard Cameron Carr was also sent to the franchise's G League affiliate ahead of its Friday night season opener against the Utah Jazz's Salt Lake City Stars.

Bronny James — in his first season in the Lakers' organization without his father, LeBron James — previously spent time in the G League, where his stat lines have often dwarfed those he has authored in the NBA.

Across a combined 25 regular-season games the past two seasons with the then-South Bay Lakers — who have since relocated and rebranded — Bronny James posted 18.4 points, 4.4 assists, 4 rebounds and 1.5 steals per game while shooting 49.6% from the field, including 41.3% from beyond the arc.

James, now 22, was the No. 55 pick when he was drafted out of USC more than two years ago. He appeared in 27 regular-season games with the Lakers as a rookie, making father-son history but struggling mightily at times, particularly early on.

He made noticeable progress in Year 2, taking the court for 42 regular-season games and raising his field-goal percentage from 31.3% (2024-25) to 40.9% (2025-26) and his 3-point percentage from 28.1% (2024-25) to 38.6% (2025-26).

Head coach JJ Redick saw enough from James to include him in his playoff rotation last season, albeit a shorthanded one.

That paved the way for additional excitement and milestones.

Coming into Year 3, the question was: How would James fare without his superstar father on the Lakers (including how would he be treated by staff and teammates in the absence of the future Hall of Famer)?

But James hasn't been given much of an opportunity in the regular season to make any kind of jump. In his sparing minutes, he scored 0.8 points per game. He attempted only three field goals and made two of them.

James could wind up back with the Lakers soon, but his lack of on-court involvement so far isn't promising, in terms of his chances of carving out the long-term 3-and-D role he teased on occasion last season.

As for Knecht, he ended up signing a two-way deal with the New York Knicks, for whom he logged 10 points on 4-of-6 shooting, plus seven rebounds, on Wednesday in a win over the Chicago Bulls.

#2953. Democratic sweep gives markets a reason to rethink path of interest rates

The Democrats' resounding victory in the U.S. midterms has the potential to curb interest rate hikes globally next year, according to market watchers — but the uncertain path of oil prices, U.S. economic growth and fiscal policies will be key.

Central banks around the world are back in monetary tightening mode, with the Federal Reserve, Bank of England, European Central Bank and Bank of Japan all raising rates in recent months.

The shift has in part stemmed from the U.S. and Israel's war with Iran driving up prices in oil and gas markets. The war has also exacerbated a squeeze in global refining capacity which has sent the cost of products like diesel, gasoline and jet fuel soaring.

Traders largely expected big Democratic gains this week after polls pointed in their favor, but there was still uncertainty leading up to the vote about whether the party would capture both chambers of Congress, increasing the ability for lawmakers to challenge President Donald Trump's policymaking.

Crude oil prices tumbled following the clear-cut Democratic victory in both the House and Senate, with international benchmark ICE Brent crude falling 8.7% on Wednesday, closing below $100 a barrel for the first time since September.

That was largely attributed to raised expectations that the result may mean Trump is more inclined to strike a deal to end the war in Iran, helping restore Gulf refinery capacity and improve export conditions. Vice President JD Vance's lengthy statement blaming Republican losses on the Iran war boosted that view.

Dan Coatsworth, head of markets at AJ Bell, said there had been "an admission from the Republican camp that the Iran war cost them a clean sweep in the election, which the market has taken to indicate that a swift resolution to the Middle East conflict could help them save face."

"Trump might relish the opportunity to take all the credit if an end to the war leads to a big drop in energy costs."

If the election result "pushes for a resolution of the conflict in the Middle East, that might bring oil down, and that's been an issue for the Fed and inflation, and part of the reason why rates are higher," said Andrew Slimmon, senior portfolio manager and head of the Applied Equity Advisors at Morgan Stanley.

Historic data shows a tight correlation between oil prices and global rates, Ralph Axel, rates strategist at Bank of America, said in a research note published Thursday. Higher oil prices over the next three to six months would likely mean "significantly higher interest rates" in the U.S., but especially so in the U.K. and Germany, he said.

"If oil prices rise, we think rates will too, especially at the front end where long-end term premium is less of a factor and monetary policy expectations play a larger role," he added.

As the uptick in crude prices on Thursday and choppiness on Friday showed, a resolution to the nearly nine-month-long conflict is far from locked in.

"For now, oil has begun to climb back up again, and interest rate expectations have not radically changed from pre-election levels," AJ Bell's Dan Coatsworth noted.

Money markets on Thursday indicated a moderate change in expectations from a week ago, both in the U.S. and Europe, with traders pricing in a slightly higher chance of monetary easing on a one-year horizon.

The effect on a two-year timeframe was more pronounced in the euro area than the U.S., according to CNBC analysis of LSEG data, with rate pricing down 8.7 basis points on the week for the ECB versus 4.6 basis points for the Fed.

"Republicans don't really have an off-ramp here at this stage," said Laurel Rapp, director for the U.S. and North America Program at Chatham House. "We know the Democrats will seek to take votes in the House and the Senate to constrain the Republicans, to restrain the administration on Iran war powers. But whether that will bind the administration is another matter."

Eurasia Group's Clayton Allen meanwhile told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that while the war with Iran is unpopular at home, leaving the Middle East region without tangible wins would also be unpopular and that he expected an escalation within weeks.

Andrew Bailey, governor of the Bank of England — which hiked rates by 25 basis points on Thursday — told CNBC that energy price pressures stemmed from a combination of obstructions on the Strait of Hormuz, a shortage in refining capacity, and low European gas storage.

"A peace process in the Middle East will not shift all of those elements," he told CNBC's Steve Sedgwick on Thursday.

"Let's wait and see, because we have seen that fall in energy prices over the last 48 hours, but it is a very volatile situation, as we've learnt over the last seven, eight, nine months."

Ken Egan, head of European Sovereign Credit at KBRA, said the interest rate outlook would be primarily impacted by whether economies have enough underlying momentum for existing inflation pressures to become more persistent.

Europe is "heading into winter after months of elevated energy prices, and the full impact is still feeding through to the wider economy," he said by email.

"The concern for central banks is whether these pressures start to push up wages and prices more broadly, or whether weaker demand limits that pass-through."

A key question for the Federal Reserve will be whether the blue sweep "could lead to more fiscal prudence in the U.S. and thus less inflation," Jane Foley, head of FX strategy at Rabobank, said by email.

"It is very early, however, to be sure of the fiscal outlook, not least because many Democrats were voted in to soften cost of living pressures ... so it is very difficult to come to a firm conclusion at this point as to how the outlook for rates will change next year," she said.

The high level of U.S. fiscal stimulus and whether it is about to change is the "million dollar question" right now, James Sullivan, managing director and head of Asia Pacific equity research at J.P. Morgan, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

"The reasons why you're seeing inflation running high is a combination of a range of factors, from the war in Iran, to tariffs, to significant tightness in particular areas of the energy complex," he said.

"But it's also important to recognize the U.S. government has continued to spend at very high levels, into an economic expansion with low unemployment, and that's driving very specific macroeconomic implications. If that changes, you potentially take the pressure off the rate environment in a way that gives the Fed significantly more forward flexibility."

Daniel Clifton, head of policy research at Baird Strategas, said the impact on the data center buildout in the U.S. was another big factor to watch.

"In 2025, we had major fiscal headwinds, and it really didn't matter because we had such a big capex boom in the AI build-out last year. This year was driven a lot by stimulus," he told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

"If you have an environment where you have tighter fiscal policy — the Fed is raising rates right now — and you have a slowdown in the AI capex build out, you're probably going to reach a policy mistake at some point in mid-2027," he said.

"That's what happened in 2000 during the Y2K tech boom, the Fed was raising rates, we had higher oil prices, and they just went too far, and eventually it had a recession. Our forecast is not for a recession in the United States. Our forecast is that we'll have a mid-cycle slowdown in 2027."

#2952. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2951. U.S. economy added 165,000 jobs in October, way more than expected; unemployment rate at 4.1%

The U.S. economy added jobs at a much stronger than expected pace in October, while the unemployment rate moved lower as the labor market showed surprising strength.

Nonfarm payrolls increased by a seasonally adjusted 165,000 for the month, compared with the Dow Jones estimate for 59,000, the Bureau of Labor Statistics reported Friday.

The unemployment rate nudged down to 4.1%, compared with the forecast for 4.2%. A more encompassing measure that includes discouraged workers and those holding part-time jobs for economic reasons rose to 7.9%, up 0.3 percentage point, amid an increase of 421,000 in the civilian labor force.

In addition to the strong payroll gains for October, prior months were revised up substantially: The August figure moved up to 186,000, an upward revision of 53,000, while the September count moved to 60,000 following an upward revision of 31,000.

Payroll gains also came in better than forecast. Average hourly earnings increased 0.4% on the month and 3.1% annually, compared to respective estimates for 0.3% and 2.9%. The average work week was unchanged at 34.4 hours.

The breadth of job growth also was strong, contrary to a trend in which health care had accounted for the bulk of gains.

Professional and business services saw an increase of 70,000 to lead all sectors, while health care was next at 42,000. Other areas seeing notable growth included transportation and warehousing (25,000) and manufacturing (19,000).
